\section{Methodology}\label{sec:Material_methods}

This section describes the methodological framework adopted to address the research objectives outlined in Section 5. The methodology integrates physics-informed deep learning architectures enhanced with cycle-consistent regularization, Bayesian hyperparameter optimization, domain adaptation strategies for sim-to-real transfer, and interpretability mechanisms for physical validation. The synthetic datasets described in Section~\ref{sec:databases} provide the controlled foundation for model development, while the experimental reference datasets guide domain adaptation and validation.

\subsection{Specific Objective 1: Cycle-Consistent Bidirectional Mapping with Degeneracy Mitigation}

This subsection addresses the first specific objective: developing a physics-informed framework for bidirectional mapping between Hamiltonian parameters and magnetic domain configurations. Its foundation is the synthetic dataset generation described in Section~\ref{sec:databases}, whose GPU-accelerated, tensor/matrix formulation of the extended Hamiltonian---one of the methodological novelties of this work---produces the labelled corpus that trains both directions of the mapping without altering the underlying physics. The matrix decomposition recasts the atomistic energy as linear-algebra operations, enabling the massively parallel simulation (checkerboard sublattice updates, independent stochastic replicas, and multi-GPU distribution of parameter sets) detailed in Section~\ref{sec:montecarlo_sim}, which reduces the wall-clock cost of dataset generation by orders of magnitude relative to sequential atomistic codes.

On this basis, the mapping is decomposed into two coupled directions. The \emph{inverse} direction is a deep regressor, trained from scratch, that estimates the Hamiltonian parameters from a magnetization image. The \emph{forward} direction is a conditional generative model (a conditional variational autoencoder and a conditional diffusion model) that synthesizes a magnetization image from the parameters. Both directions are closed into a cycle-consistent loop and validated with physically grounded metrics; Bayesian hyper-parameter tuning is used only as an auxiliary component to balance the competing training objectives while mitigating degeneracy effects.

\subsubsection{Deep Regression Framework for Hamiltonian Parameter Estimation}\label{sec:deep_regression}

The inverse estimation of the Hamiltonian parameters and thermodynamic state from magnetic domain images is formalized as a continuous mapping problem. Given the generated dataset $\mathcal{D}$, where $\mathbf{X}_n$ represents the normalized $s_z$ spatial magnetization field and $\boldsymbol{\theta}_n$ represents the vector of generating physical parameters, we aim to learn a parameterized non-linear mapping function $f_\Theta : \mathbb{R}^{\breve{H} \times \breve{W}} \rightarrow \mathbb{R}^{\breve{P}}$, to find the predicted parameters $\hat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}_n\in\mathbb{R}^{\breve{P}}.$

Here, $f_\Theta$ denotes a deep neural network architecture parameterized by a set of learnable weights and biases $\Theta$. The optimal parameter set $\hat{\Theta}$ is obtained by minimizing a continuous loss function over the training distribution~\cite{murphy2023probabilistic}:

\begin{equation}\label{eq:LossFunction}
\hat{\Theta} = \arg\min_{\Theta} \frac{1}{\breve{N}} \sum_{n=1}^{\breve{N}} \mathcal{L}\left(\boldsymbol{\theta}_n, f_\Theta(\mathbf{X}_n)\right),
\end{equation}
where $\mathcal{L}$ represents the chosen regression objective, typically the Mean Squared Error (MSE) or Huber loss, which mitigates the influence of outlier topological states during optimization. By minimizing this objective, the network intrinsically learns to untangle the highly multimodal spatial features mapping to the specific energetic balances of the extended Heisenberg Hamiltonian.

A recurring practice in prior deep-learning approaches to this inverse problem is to adopt, by default, a single heavy off-the-shelf vision architecture---most often a deep residual network or an Xception-style backbone---without examining whether such capacity is actually warranted for magnetic-texture regression or comparing it against lighter alternatives. The magnetic-imaging inverse problem, however, lives on a much narrower manifold than natural images, so this default is far from self-evident. Turning this unexamined choice into an explicit object of study is part of the contribution of this work: rather than committing to one backbone, the framework evaluates and compares a lightweight convolutional baseline (a conventional CNN) against four more complex, state-of-the-art architectures---ResNet50, DenseNet, Xception, and the Vision Transformer (ViT)---to determine how much architectural capacity the task genuinely requires. All models are trained \emph{from scratch}, deliberately avoiding transfer learning: the magnetic textures are already well-defined at the input, and---consistent with a reviewer's observation---pretraining on natural-image corpora (e.g., ImageNet) proved not to contribute to this task, whose statistics differ fundamentally from those of everyday photographs. The conventional CNN baseline directly tests whether the additional capacity of the deeper backbones is required at all, and the comparison is later read jointly with model size and interpretability to characterize the accuracy-versus-capacity trade-off.

\begin{itemize}

\item[--] \emph{Convolutional Baseline (conventional CNN)}: A compact network trained from scratch on the native $s_z$ images, comprising a short stack of convolutional stages (convolution, batch normalization, ReLU, and spatial pooling) followed by global average pooling and a dense regression head. It serves as a reference against which the added representational capacity of the deeper backbones is measured.

\item[--] \emph{Residual Networks (ResNet50)}~\cite{koonce2021resnet}: Standard deep convolutional networks often suffer from the vanishing gradient problem as depth increases, hindering the learning of complex physical mappings. ResNet50 circumvents this by introducing shortcut connections that bypass multiple convolutional layers, directly propagating spatial information and gradients. Mathematically, if $\mathbf{x}$ is the input to a sequence of layers, and $\mathcal{F}(\mathbf{x}, \{\mathbf{W}_l\})$ represents the residual mapping to be learned (comprising convolutions, batch normalization, and ReLU activations) for the $l$-th layer, the output $\mathbf{y}$ of the residual block is defined as:

\begin{equation}\label{eq:ResNet}
\mathbf{y} = \mathcal{F}(\mathbf{x}, \{\mathbf{W}_l\}) + \mathbf{x}.
\end{equation}

In the context of magnetic domain images, these skip connections allow the network to simultaneously preserve low-level spatial features (like abrupt chiral boundaries) while composing deep, high-level abstractions of the global thermodynamic state (see Figure~\ref{fig:resnet_implementation}).

%ResNet50
\begin{figure}[H]
    \centering
    \resizebox{\linewidth}{!}{
    \input{Figures/ResNet50}}
\caption{ResNet50 architecture pipeline for Hamiltonian parameter estimation. The pipeline progresses from single-channel input preprocessing through a convolutional feature extractor trained from scratch. The Residual bottleneck structure used extensively in Conv5\_x. The deepest features are processed by the customized regression head containing a Global Average Pooling (GAP) and a final dense layer with linear activation to output the predicted physical constants.}
\label{fig:resnet_implementation}
\end{figure}

\item[--] \emph{Densely Connected Convolutional Networks (DenseNet)}~\cite{huang2017densely}: While ResNet adds features via summation, DenseNet concatenates them, maximizing information flow and feature reuse throughout the network. This is particularly advantageous for the Hamiltonian inverse problem, as the delicate energetic competition often manifests in subtle, overlapping spatial frequencies that require dense multi-scale analysis. In a DenseNet block, the feature maps of all preceding layers are used as inputs to the current layer. If $\mathbf{x}_l$ denotes the output of the $l$-th layer, and $H_l(\cdot)$ represents a composite function of batch normalization, ReLU, and convolution, the forward pass is expressed as:

\begin{equation}\label{eq:DenseNet}
\mathbf{x}_l = H_l([\mathbf{x}_0, \mathbf{x}_1, \dots, \mathbf{x}_{l-1}]),
\end{equation}
where $[\dots]$ denotes the concatenation operation. This dense topological structure ensures that the final regression head has direct access to both fine-grained pixel interactions and coarse global structures (see Figure~\ref{fig:densenet_implementation}).

\begin{figure}[H]
    \centering
    \resizebox{\linewidth}{!}{
    \input{Figures/DenseNet}}
\caption{DenseNet121 architecture pipeline for Hamiltonian parameter estimation. The pipeline progresses left-to-right, processing single-channel inputs through the feature extractor composed of Dense Blocks and Transition layers. A detailed horizontal view illustrates the dense connectivity mechanism where feature maps from all preceding layers are concatenated before convolution. The final aggregated features are mapped to physical constants via the customized regression head.}
\label{fig:densenet_implementation}
\end{figure}


\item[--] \emph{Xception (Extreme Inception)}~\cite{chollet2017xception}: This architecture operates on the hypothesis that cross-channel correlations and spatial correlations in feature maps can be entirely decoupled. It replaces standard Inception modules with depthwise separable convolutions, drastically reducing computational overhead while enhancing representational efficiency. A depthwise separable convolution splits the standard convolution into two distinct steps. First, a depthwise convolution applies a single spatial filter to each input channel independently. Second, a pointwise convolution ($1 \times 1$ convolution) projects the output of the depthwise step onto a new channel space to capture cross-channel correlations. If $\mathbf{X} \in \mathbb{R}^{\breve{H} \times \breve{W} \times \breve{C}}$ is the input tensor, the operation is formally approximated as:

\begin{equation}\label{eq:Xception}
\text{Conv}_{\text{sep}}(\mathbf{X}) = \text{Conv}_{\text{pointwise}}(\text{Conv}_{\text{depthwise}}(\mathbf{X})).
\end{equation}

For nanoscale magnetism, this extreme decoupling allows the network to efficiently isolate specific topological frequency patterns (depthwise) before correlating them to the complex parameter space $\boldsymbol{\theta}_n$ (see Figure~\ref{fig:xception_implementation}).

\begin{figure}[H]
    \centering
    \resizebox{0.85\linewidth}{!}{
    \input{Figures/xception}}
\caption{Xception architecture pipeline for Hamiltonian parameter estimation. The feature extractor composed of Entry, Middle, and Exit flows. A detailed horizontal view illustrates the depthwise separable convolution mechanism where spatial feature extraction (depthwise) and cross-channel correlations (pointwise) are entirely decoupled. The final aggregated features are mapped to physical constants.}\label{fig:xception_implementation}
\end{figure}



\item[--] \emph{Visual Transformer (ViT)}~\cite{dosovitskiy2020image}: Unlike Convolutional Neural Networks, which enforce a strict local inductive bias, the Vision Transformer processes the 2D spatial magnetization field $\mathbf{X}_n$ as a sequence of flattened, non-overlapping 2D patches. This allows the model to capture long-range, global dependencies across the nanodot instantly, which is critical for understanding macro-scale topological stability dictated by the Zeeman field or global anisotropy. The input image is divided into $N_p$ patches, which are linearly projected into a sequence of $D$-dimensional embeddings. The core mechanism is the Multi-Head Self-Attention (MSA), which computes attention scores to weigh the importance of different patches relative to one another. For a given set of queries $Q$, keys $K$, and values $V$ derived from the patch embeddings, the scaled dot-product attention is defined as:

\begin{equation}\label{eq:ViT}
\text{Attention}(Q, K, V) = \text{softmax}\left(\frac{QK^\top}{\sqrt{d_k}}\right)V,
\end{equation}
where $d_k$ is the scaling factor (the dimension of the keys). By employing MSA, the ViT can explicitly correlate a localized structural defect on one edge of the nanodot with a compensatory chiral boundary on the opposite edge, mapping these distant correlations directly to the global DMI or symmetric exchange strengths.

\begin{figure}[H]
    \centering
    \resizebox{\linewidth}{!}{
    \input{Figures/ViT}}
\caption{ViT architecture pipeline for Hamiltonian parameter estimation. The pipeline progresses left-to-right, processing single-channel inputs through patch extraction, linear projection, and a sequential backbone of Transformer Encoder blocks. The core Transformer block mechanism, highlighting the MSA that explicitly models long-range spatial dependencies across the magnetic nanodot. The final sequence of high-level token representations is aggregated via 1D GAP and mapped to the physical constants through the customized regression head.}
\label{fig:vit_implementation}
\end{figure}


To optimize the parameter set $\hat{\Theta}$ in Equation~\ref{eq:LossFunction} across all evaluated architectures, the models are trained utilizing the MSE as the explicit continuous loss function $\mathcal{L}$. The optimization process is executed using the Adam optimizer, relying on backpropagation and automatic differentiation to efficiently compute the network gradients~\cite{murakami2022inverse}. By iteratively updating the model weights based on these computed gradients, the training pipeline systematically minimizes the structural and energetic discrepancies between the predicted outputs and the true Hamiltonian constants, ensuring robust convergence in estimating the underlying physical parameters.

\end{itemize}


\paragraph{Training protocol and data augmentation.}
All architectures are trained from scratch under an identical protocol---the same optimizer, learning-rate schedule, early-stopping criterion, and regression loss (Equation~\ref{eq:LossFunction})---so that the comparison isolates the effect of the architecture rather than the training regime. To improve generalization and exploit the symmetries of the problem, the dataset is enriched on-the-fly with \emph{geometric augmentations that preserve the target parameters}: since the eight Hamiltonian constants are scalar quantities invariant to rigid motions of the nanodot, $90^{\circ}$ rotations and translations of the $s_z$ image leave the labels unchanged and are therefore applied as label-preserving augmentations. Reflections, by contrast, are deliberately excluded: a mirror operation reverses the in-plane chirality and thus corresponds to a sign change of the antisymmetric (DMI) coupling, so it would relabel the sample rather than augment it.

\paragraph{Integration into the Cycle-Consistent Framework.}
This deep regression model constitutes the inverse pathway $f_{\hat{\Theta}}$ of the cycle-consistent framework (Section~\ref{sec:cycle_consistency}), where it is coupled with the forward conditional generative model. By enforcing round-trip parameter consistency --- recovering the Hamiltonian parameters from the generated magnetic state --- the framework constrains the intrinsic degeneracy of the inverse problem and steers it toward a single, physically consistent solution.

\subsubsection{Conditional Generative Models for the Forward Map}\label{sec:cvae_forward}

The forward pathway learns the conditional generative map $g_{\Phi}:\boldsymbol{\theta}\mapsto\mathbf{X}$, which synthesizes a magnetic domain image $\mathbf{X}\in[0,1]^{\breve{H}\times\breve{W}}$ from a Hamiltonian parameter vector $\boldsymbol{\theta}$, thereby inverting the direction of the regression model $f_{\hat{\Theta}}$ of Section~\ref{sec:deep_regression}. Because the forward map is intrinsically one-to-many --- at finite temperature, many thermally relaxed textures are compatible with a given $\boldsymbol{\theta}$ --- it is modeled probabilistically as $p_{\Phi}(\mathbf{X}\,|\,\boldsymbol{\theta})$. We consider two candidate families of conditional generators, both conditioned on the physical parameters $\boldsymbol{\theta}$: a conditional variational autoencoder (cVAE) and a conditional denoising diffusion model (DDPM). These are treated as \emph{alternatives} rather than jointly deployed: a single forward generator is retained for the cycle, selected as whichever attains the better fidelity--consistency trade-off; preliminary experiments already favor the diffusion model, which we therefore adopt as the primary candidate while keeping the cVAE as a baseline.

\paragraph{Conditional Variational Autoencoder (cVAE).}
The cVAE introduces a latent variable $\mathbf{z}\in\mathbb{R}^{d_z}$ and couples three networks: a posterior (encoder) $q(\mathbf{z}\,|\,\mathbf{X},\boldsymbol{\theta})$, a learned conditional prior $p(\mathbf{z}\,|\,\boldsymbol{\theta})$, and a decoder that reconstructs $\hat{\mathbf{X}}$ from $(\mathbf{z},\boldsymbol{\theta})$. The encoder shares the convolutional structure of one of the inverse backbones of Section~\ref{sec:deep_regression} (e.g., Xception or ViT), trained from scratch for this task, whose pooled features are concatenated with a learned embedding of $\boldsymbol{\theta}$ to produce a diagonal Gaussian posterior,
\begin{equation}
q(\mathbf{z}\,|\,\mathbf{X},\boldsymbol{\theta}) = \mathcal{N}\!\big(\mathbf{z};\,\boldsymbol{\mu}_q(\mathbf{X},\boldsymbol{\theta}),\,\mathrm{diag}\,\boldsymbol{\sigma}_q^2(\mathbf{X},\boldsymbol{\theta})\big),\qquad
\mathbf{z} = \boldsymbol{\mu}_q + \boldsymbol{\sigma}_q\odot\boldsymbol{\epsilon},\quad \boldsymbol{\epsilon}\sim\mathcal{N}(\mathbf{0},\mathbf{I}),
\label{eq:cvae_posterior}
\end{equation}
where the second expression is the reparameterization used for low-variance gradient estimation. Unlike the classical VAE, which fixes an uninformative prior $\mathcal{N}(\mathbf{0},\mathbf{I})$, the latent space is structured by a \emph{learned conditional prior} driven by the physical parameters,
\begin{equation}
p(\mathbf{z}\,|\,\boldsymbol{\theta}) = \mathcal{N}\!\big(\mathbf{z};\,\boldsymbol{\mu}_p(\boldsymbol{\theta}),\,\mathrm{diag}\,\boldsymbol{\sigma}_p^2(\boldsymbol{\theta})\big),
\label{eq:cvae_prior}
\end{equation}
so that distinct parameter regimes are mapped to distinct latent regions, discouraging the collapse of physically different states into overlapping codes. The model is trained by minimizing a reconstruction term --- combining structural similarity (SSIM) and an $L_1$ penalty computed between the target and reconstructed images --- regularized by the Kullback--Leibler (KL) divergence between posterior and conditional prior,
\begin{equation}
\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{cVAE}} = \underbrace{\big(1-\mathrm{SSIM}(\mathbf{X},\hat{\mathbf{X}})\big) + \lambda_{L_1}\,\lVert \mathbf{X}-\hat{\mathbf{X}}\rVert_1}_{\text{reconstruction}} \;+\; \beta\,D_{\mathrm{KL}}\!\big(q(\mathbf{z}\,|\,\mathbf{X},\boldsymbol{\theta})\,\|\,p(\mathbf{z}\,|\,\boldsymbol{\theta})\big).
\label{eq:cvae_total_loss}
\end{equation}
For the two diagonal Gaussians the KL term admits the closed form
\begin{equation}
D_{\mathrm{KL}}\!\big(q\,\|\,p\big) = \frac{1}{2}\sum_{k=1}^{d_z}\left[\log\frac{\sigma_{p,k}^2}{\sigma_{q,k}^2} + \frac{\sigma_{q,k}^2 + (\mu_{q,k}-\mu_{p,k})^2}{\sigma_{p,k}^2} - 1\right].
\label{eq:cvae_kl}
\end{equation}
The weight $\beta$ follows a warmup--plateau schedule (annealed from a near-zero value to a fixed $\beta_{\max}$) to prevent posterior collapse. At inference, generation is purely conditional: a latent code is drawn from the prior, $\mathbf{z}\sim p(\mathbf{z}\,|\,\boldsymbol{\theta})$, and decoded together with $\boldsymbol{\theta}$ to yield $\hat{\mathbf{X}}$. Figure~\ref{fig:cvae_diagram} summarizes the architecture.

\begin{figure}[H]
\centering

\paragraph{Conditional Denoising Diffusion Model (DDPM).}
The diffusion model defines a fixed forward process that gradually corrupts a clean image $\mathbf{x}_0=\mathbf{X}$ with Gaussian noise over $T$ steps according to a variance schedule $\{\beta_t\}_{t=1}^{T}$ (a linear schedule with $T=1000$ is used). Writing $\alpha_t = 1-\beta_t$ and $\bar{\alpha}_t = \prod_{s=1}^{t}\alpha_s$, the marginal at step $t$ has the closed form
\begin{equation}
q(\mathbf{x}_t\,|\,\mathbf{x}_0) = \mathcal{N}\!\big(\mathbf{x}_t;\,\sqrt{\bar{\alpha}_t}\,\mathbf{x}_0,\,(1-\bar{\alpha}_t)\mathbf{I}\big),\qquad
\mathbf{x}_t = \sqrt{\bar{\alpha}_t}\,\mathbf{x}_0 + \sqrt{1-\bar{\alpha}_t}\,\boldsymbol{\epsilon},\;\;\boldsymbol{\epsilon}\sim\mathcal{N}(\mathbf{0},\mathbf{I}).
\label{eq:ddpm_forward}
\end{equation}
A conditional U-Net $\boldsymbol{\epsilon}_{\Phi}(\mathbf{x}_t,t,\boldsymbol{\theta})$ is trained to predict the injected noise given the noisy image, the timestep $t$, and the physical parameters $\boldsymbol{\theta}$, by minimizing the denoising objective
\begin{equation}
\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{DDPM}} = \mathbb{E}_{t,\,\mathbf{x}_0,\,\boldsymbol{\epsilon}}\Big[\,\big\lVert \boldsymbol{\epsilon} - \boldsymbol{\epsilon}_{\Phi}(\mathbf{x}_t,t,\boldsymbol{\theta})\big\rVert^2\,\Big],\qquad t\sim\mathcal{U}\{1,\dots,T\}.
\label{eq:ddpm_loss}
\end{equation}
\emph{Conditioning mechanism (the core novelty).} The way in which the parameter vector enters the denoiser is central to this work. Whereas prior diffusion models for spin systems condition only on a single external scalar---typically the temperature, an ambient control that is not part of the Hamiltonian---here the network is conditioned on the \emph{full} eight-dimensional vector $\boldsymbol{\theta}=[T^{(0)},\tilde{J}_2,\tilde{J}_3,\tilde{J}_4,\tilde{K}_{an1},\tilde{K}_{anS},\tilde{H}_{ex},\tilde{K}_{DM}]$ that generates each configuration, exactly as defined for the dataset in Section~\ref{sec:training_datasets}. The conditioning is built as follows. The timestep is mapped to a sinusoidal positional encoding and the parameter vector to a learned embedding, each passed through a two-layer perceptron:
\begin{equation}\label{eq:cond_embed}
\mathbf{e}_t = \mathrm{MLP}_t\big(\mathrm{PE}(t)\big),\qquad
\mathbf{e}_{\boldsymbol{\theta}} = \mathrm{MLP}_{\boldsymbol{\theta}}(\boldsymbol{\theta}) \;\in\; \mathbb{R}^{d_e},
\end{equation}
where $\mathrm{PE}(\cdot)$ denotes the sinusoidal encoding. These two signals play deliberately \emph{distinct} roles inside every residual block of the conditional U-Net. The temporal embedding, which only tells the network \emph{how much} noise remains, is injected \emph{additively}; the physical-parameter embedding, which must reshape the very content of the texture, instead modulates the features through a per-channel affine (feature-wise) conditioning. Concretely, for a feature map $\mathbf{h}^{(\ell)}\in\mathbb{R}^{C_\ell\times H_\ell\times W_\ell}$ at level $\ell$, a level-specific affine projection of $\mathbf{e}_{\boldsymbol{\theta}}$ produces a per-channel scale $\boldsymbol{\gamma}^{(\ell)}$ and shift $\boldsymbol{\beta}^{(\ell)}$,
\begin{equation}\label{eq:cond_film}
\begin{aligned}
\mathbf{h}^{(\ell)} &\;\leftarrow\; \mathrm{Norm}\!\big(\mathbf{h}^{(\ell)}\big) + \big(\mathbf{W}_t^{(\ell)}\mathbf{e}_t\big)\otimes\mathbf{1}_{H_\ell\times W_\ell}, \\[2pt]
\big(\boldsymbol{\gamma}^{(\ell)},\,\boldsymbol{\beta}^{(\ell)}\big) &= \mathbf{W}_{\boldsymbol{\theta}}^{(\ell)}\mathbf{e}_{\boldsymbol{\theta}} + \mathbf{b}^{(\ell)}, \qquad
\mathbf{h}^{(\ell)} \;\leftarrow\; \boldsymbol{\gamma}^{(\ell)}\odot\mathbf{h}^{(\ell)} + \boldsymbol{\beta}^{(\ell)},
\end{aligned}
\end{equation}
where $\odot$ denotes per-channel (broadcast) multiplication, so that the physical conditioning \emph{multiplicatively gates} the denoiser at every resolution rather than merely adding a bias. Because $\mathbf{e}_{\boldsymbol{\theta}}$ encodes the \emph{joint} effect of the eight couplings rather than a single control variable, the denoiser $\boldsymbol{\epsilon}_{\Phi}(\mathbf{x}_t,t,\boldsymbol{\theta})$ must learn the genuine multi-parameter map from the Hamiltonian to the texture---precisely the regime in which parameter degeneracy arises---distinguishing this formulation from single-scalar conditional generators.

New magnetic states are produced by reversing the process: starting from pure noise $\mathbf{x}_T\sim\mathcal{N}(\mathbf{0},\mathbf{I})$ and iterating, for $t=T,\dots,1$,
\begin{equation}
\mathbf{x}_{t-1} = \frac{1}{\sqrt{\alpha_t}}\left(\mathbf{x}_t - \frac{\beta_t}{\sqrt{1-\bar{\alpha}_t}}\,\boldsymbol{\epsilon}_{\Phi}(\mathbf{x}_t,t,\boldsymbol{\theta})\right) + \sigma_t\,\mathbf{z},\qquad \mathbf{z}\sim\mathcal{N}(\mathbf{0},\mathbf{I}),
\label{eq:ddpm_sampling}
\end{equation}
which yields the generated image $\hat{\mathbf{X}}=\mathbf{x}_0$ conditioned on $\boldsymbol{\theta}$. Figure~\ref{fig:ddpm_diagram} illustrates the forward corruption and the conditional reverse denoising.

\paragraph{Physically grounded evaluation.}
Beyond image-fidelity scores, the generated states are assessed with thermodynamic observables computed from the magnetization field and compared against the reference configurations. For an ensemble of $R$ states $\{\mathbf{S}^{(r)}\}_{r=1}^{R}$ generated for a given parameter set $\boldsymbol{\theta}$ at temperature $T$, and writing $\langle\,\cdot\,\rangle = \tfrac{1}{R}\sum_{r}(\cdot)$ for the ensemble (thermal) average over the $N$ lattice sites, the observables are defined as follows.

The \emph{total energy} is the ensemble average of the Hamiltonian,
\begin{equation}\label{eq:obs_energy}
E \;=\; \big\langle \mathcal{H}(\mathbf{S}) \big\rangle ,
\end{equation}
with $\mathcal{H}$ given by Equation~\ref{eq:Hamiltonian}. The \emph{net magnetization} per spin and its magnitude are
\begin{equation}\label{eq:obs_mag}
\mathbf{M} \;=\; \frac{1}{N}\sum_{i=1}^{N}\mathbf{s}_i ,
\qquad
|M| \;=\; \big\langle \lVert \mathbf{M} \rVert_2 \big\rangle ,
\end{equation}
which quantify the degree of collinear order (with $|M|\!\to\!1$ for a ferromagnetic state and $|M|\!\to\!0$ for a disordered or fully compensated texture). The response functions follow from the fluctuation--dissipation relations: the \emph{magnetic susceptibility} measures the variance of the magnetization,
\begin{equation}\label{eq:obs_chi}
\chi \;=\; \frac{N}{k_B T}\Big( \big\langle \lVert\mathbf{M}\rVert_2^{2} \big\rangle - \big\langle \lVert\mathbf{M}\rVert_2 \big\rangle^{2} \Big),
\end{equation}
and the \emph{specific heat} measures the variance of the energy,
\begin{equation}\label{eq:obs_cv}
C_v \;=\; \frac{1}{N k_B T^{2}}\Big( \big\langle \mathcal{H}^{2} \big\rangle - \big\langle \mathcal{H} \big\rangle^{2} \Big),
\end{equation}
where $k_B$ is the Boltzmann constant. Because $\chi$ and $C_v$ are built from \emph{fluctuations}, they require an ensemble of samples per parameter set rather than a single image, and they peak sharply near magnetic phase transitions---making them stringent probes of whether a generator reproduces the correct thermodynamics rather than an isolated snapshot.

Each observable $O\in\{E,\,|M|,\,\chi,\,C_v\}$ computed on the generated ensemble, $O_{\mathrm{gen}}$, is then compared with the reference value $O_{\mathrm{ref}}$ obtained from the Monte Carlo simulation through the relative error $\varepsilon_O = |O_{\mathrm{gen}}-O_{\mathrm{ref}}|/(|O_{\mathrm{ref}}|+\epsilon)$. Together with conditioning on $\boldsymbol{\theta}$ and the closed-loop recovery of parameters from generated images (Section~\ref{sec:cycle_consistency}), this physical assessment constrains the forward generator to remain thermodynamically consistent rather than merely visually plausible.

\subsubsection{Cycle-Consistent Forward--Inverse Framework}\label{sec:cycle_consistency}

The inverse estimation of Hamiltonian parameters is intrinsically ill-posed: at finite temperature many magnetic textures are compatible with a given parameter set, so a purely image-based inversion cannot, on its own, single out a unique solution. The proposed framework closes the loop between the two models developed above to resolve this ambiguity. Both models already carry the physical parameters \emph{intrinsically} --- the forward generator is conditioned on $\boldsymbol{\theta}$, and the inverse estimator is trained to predict $\boldsymbol{\theta}$ from an image --- so we treat them as two complete, already-trained blocks, whose internal formulations were detailed in Sections~\ref{sec:deep_regression} and~\ref{sec:cvae_forward}, and compose them into a closed cycle.

\paragraph{Building blocks.}
\begin{itemize}
\item[--] \emph{Inverse model} $f_{\hat{\Theta}}:\mathbf{X}\mapsto\hat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}$ (Section~\ref{sec:deep_regression}): the deep regression network that estimates the parameters from a magnetic domain image.
\item[--] \emph{Forward model} $g_{\Phi}:\boldsymbol{\theta}\mapsto\hat{\mathbf{X}}$ (Section~\ref{sec:cvae_forward}): the conditional generator (cVAE or DDPM) that synthesizes a magnetic state from the parameters.
\end{itemize}

\paragraph{Closed cycle.}
Starting from a parameter vector $\boldsymbol{\theta}$, the forward model generates a magnetic state and the inverse model attempts to recover the generating parameters from that synthetic state,
\begin{equation}
\boldsymbol{\theta}\;\xrightarrow{\;g_{\Phi}\;}\;\hat{\mathbf{X}}\;\xrightarrow{\;f_{\hat{\Theta}}\;}\;\hat{\boldsymbol{\theta}},
\label{eq:cycle_flow}
\end{equation}
and the round-trip consistency is quantified by the parameter recovery error
\begin{equation}
\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{cycle}}(\boldsymbol{\theta}) = \big\lVert\, f_{\hat{\Theta}}\big(g_{\Phi}(\boldsymbol{\theta})\big) - \boldsymbol{\theta}\,\big\rVert_2^2 .
\label{eq:cycle_consistency}
\end{equation}
The composition is illustrated in Figure~\ref{fig:cycle_consistency}.

\begin{figure}[H]
\centering

\caption{\textbf{Cycle-consistent forward--inverse framework.} The forward generator $g_{\Phi}$ maps parameters to a magnetic state, and the inverse estimator $f_{\hat{\Theta}}$ recovers the parameters from that generated state. Enforcing $\hat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}\!\approx\!\boldsymbol{\theta}$ closes the loop and drives the degenerate inverse problem toward a single, parameter-identifiable solution.}
\label{fig:cycle_consistency}
\end{figure}

\paragraph{Training strategy.}
Crucially, the cycle-consistency term of Equation~\ref{eq:cycle_consistency} is not a passive diagnostic but an active \emph{training} objective: minimizing $\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{cycle}}$ back-propagates through both blocks and jointly re-adjusts them, which is precisely the mechanism that steers the degenerate inverse problem toward a single self-consistent solution. Training therefore proceeds in two phases. First, each block is \emph{warm-started} independently under its own native objective---the inverse regressor under the parameter-regression loss (Equation~\ref{eq:LossFunction}) and the retained forward generator under its generative loss---to reach a competent initialization. Second, the two blocks are \emph{coupled and fine-tuned jointly} by adding the cycle term, minimizing
\begin{equation}\label{eq:cycle_total}
\mathcal{L} \;=\; \mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{gen}} \;+\; \lambda_{\mathrm{cyc}}\,\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{cycle}},
\end{equation}
where $\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{gen}}$ is the native loss of the chosen generator and $\lambda_{\mathrm{cyc}}$ weights the round-trip consistency. It is worth emphasizing that this objective is deliberately \emph{compact}: earlier formulations that additionally combined a physics-informed (PINN-type) energy penalty and an adversarial (GAN-type) domain-adaptation term have been discarded, since such a many-term loss of disparate scales is prone to gradient conflict and to the dominance of a single term. What remains---one generative loss and one cycle term of comparable scale---keeps the joint fine-tuning stable while preserving the degeneracy-resolving gradient coupling.

\jc{Following the examiners' recommendation to make the reconstruction objective physically---rather than pixel---grounded, and without reintroducing a many-term loss, the compact objective is complemented by a \emph{single}, small physics regularizer built from rotation- and translation-invariant global observables:}
\begin{equation}\label{eq:phys_reg}
\jc{\mathcal{L} \;=\; \mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{gen}} \;+\; \lambda_{\mathrm{cyc}}\,\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{cycle}} \;+\; \lambda_{\mathrm{phys}}\,\underbrace{\sum_{O\in\{M,\,|M|,\,k^{\ast}\}} \big\lvert\, O(\hat{\mathbf{X}}) - O(\mathbf{X})\,\big\rvert}_{\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{phys}}},}
\end{equation}
\jc{where $M$ and $|M|$ are the signed and absolute magnetization and $k^{\ast}$ is the peak wavevector of the structure factor---all differentiable, geometry-invariant scalars---and $\lambda_{\mathrm{phys}}\!\ll\!1$ is kept deliberately small so that the term merely \emph{nudges} the generator toward the correct physical statistics without recreating the gradient-dominance and scale-conflict pathologies of the discarded multi-term loss. The spin--spin correlation function is intentionally \emph{excluded} from the objective and reserved for held-out evaluation (Section~\ref{sec:ddpm_poc}), where a sliding-window variant additionally probes the \emph{local} consistency of the generated textures. This retains a single lightweight physical term in training while the richer correlation descriptors remain unbiased validators.}

{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\paragraph{Partial mitigation of degeneracy, and the temporal complement.}
Because the forward map is one-to-many, a given $\boldsymbol{\theta}$ admits many plausible textures while a given texture may be explained by several parameter sets. Requiring that a synthesized state map back to its own generating parameters acts as a selection principle: among the degenerate candidates, the cycle actively steers the inverse estimator toward one mutually consistent solution, rather than letting it saturate to the blurred, degeneracy-averaged prediction that an unconstrained regressor tends to produce. This is a deliberate design goal of the cycle---to \emph{push} the ill-conditioned problem toward a single, self-consistent configuration instead of an uninformative mean.

It must be stressed, however, that this mitigation is only \emph{partial}. When the ambiguity is intrinsic to the observation---for instance, distinct parameter sets that relax into genuinely indistinguishable single-axis ($s_z$) textures---no image-based constraint, cycle consistency included, can recover the missing information from a single static state. Resolving these residual cases requires supplying \emph{additional} physical information, which is precisely the role of the second specific objective of this work: rather than a single equilibrium image, it exploits the \emph{trajectory} of states produced by varying the ambient conditions (temperature and field)---the ordered sequence of images behaving like a short ``video'' of the transition---so that \emph{how} a texture evolves, and not only its final form, becomes discriminative of the underlying couplings. That temporal, diffusion- and information-based strategy, still under active development, is therefore complementary to the cycle-consistent framework developed here: the cycle removes the degeneracy that is resolvable from equilibrium images, while the transition dynamics are aimed at the degeneracy that is not.}

\paragraph{Closed-loop evaluation.}
The cycle is assessed on held-out parameter sets at three complementary levels, with the analysis stratified by the magnetic-phase clusters identified in the latent space (ferromagnetic, helical, labyrinthine, and skyrmionic regimes), so that identifiability can be reported per physical regime:
\begin{itemize}
\item[--] \emph{Image level:} agreement between the generated and reference states through \emph{symmetry-tolerant} measures---primarily the correlation of their Fourier power spectra, which is invariant to translation (and, under radial averaging, to rotation), complemented by the structural-similarity index (SSIM). Pixel-wise error such as MSE is deliberately \emph{not} used at this level: magnetic textures are physically invariant to rigid motions, so MSE would unfairly penalize rotated or translated but physically equivalent states and bias the generator toward a single averaged output.
\item[--] \emph{Physical level:} consistency of physical observables computed on the nanodot --- magnetization $M$ and $|M|$, magnetic susceptibility $\chi$, specific heat $C_v$, and total energy $E$.
\item[--] \emph{Parameter level:} the recovery error between the original $\boldsymbol{\theta}$ and the re-estimated $\hat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}$, which directly measures how well the loop resolves the degeneracy.
\end{itemize}
A small parameter-level error together with physically consistent observables indicates that the forward and inverse blocks are mutually coherent and that the framework has steered the inverse problem toward a unique, physically grounded solution.

\subsubsection{Bayesian Hyperparameter Optimization}

Hyper-parameter tuning is treated here as an \emph{auxiliary}, well-scoped step rather than as a central component of the contribution, and its design deliberately sidesteps a concern that a costly, indiscriminate search over a large composite objective would raise. As established in the training strategy above, the framework does not optimize a single monolithic loss balancing many competing terms: the physics-informed (PINN-type) and adversarial (GAN-type) terms of earlier formulations have been removed, and each block is warm-started under its own native loss before a joint fine-tuning phase governed by the compact objective of Equation~\ref{eq:cycle_total}. Consequently, tuning does not attempt to jointly balance a high-dimensional weighting of disparate loss terms; instead, each module exposes only a \emph{small} set of critical hyperparameters $\boldsymbol{\lambda}$---for the retained generator, essentially the latent dimensionality and KL weight $\beta$ (cVAE) or the noise schedule and depth (diffusion), together with the learning rate and the single cycle weight $\lambda_{\mathrm{cyc}}$.

Over this low-dimensional space, Bayesian optimization is an efficient and principled alternative to manual or grid search:
\begin{equation}
\label{eq:hyperparameter_objective}
\boldsymbol{\lambda}^{\star} =
\arg\min_{\boldsymbol{\lambda} \in \Lambda}
\mathcal{J}(\boldsymbol{\lambda}),
\end{equation}
where $\mathcal{J}$ is a per-module validation objective measured on held-out data (parameter-recovery accuracy and consistency of the physical observables). Feasibility is ensured by the low dimensionality of $\Lambda$ and by evaluating each candidate with a cheap proxy---a reduced-epoch run on a validation subset---so that the surrogate-guided search converges within a modest budget rather than incurring the prohibitive cost of exhaustively retraining the full pipeline.

\paragraph{Surrogate Modeling of the Objective.}
Instead of directly evaluating $\mathcal{J}(\boldsymbol{\lambda})$ for every configuration, Bayesian optimization constructs a probabilistic surrogate model $M$—typically a Gaussian Process (GP)—to approximate the unknown objective function.  
Given a set of observed evaluations 
$\mathcal{D}_{t} = \{ (\boldsymbol{\lambda}_{i}, y_{i}) \}_{i=1}^{t}$,
the GP surrogate models the objective as:
\begin{equation}
\label{eq:gaussian_process_surrogate}
\mathcal{J}(\boldsymbol{\lambda}) \sim 
\mathcal{GP}\big( m(\boldsymbol{\lambda}),\, 
k(\boldsymbol{\lambda}, \boldsymbol{\lambda}') \big),
\end{equation}
where $m$ is the mean function (often taken as zero) and $k$ is a covariance kernel (e.g., Matérn or squared exponential).  
The GP posterior provides both a predicted mean $\mu_{t}(\boldsymbol{\lambda})$ and uncertainty $\sigma_{t}(\boldsymbol{\lambda})$, enabling guided exploration.

\paragraph{Acquisition Function.}
Bayesian optimization selects the next hyperparameter candidate by maximizing an acquisition function $\alpha(\boldsymbol{\lambda}; \mathcal{D}_{t})$ that balances exploration and exploitation.  
A commonly used acquisition function is *Expected Improvement* (EI), defined as:
\begin{equation}
\label{eq:expected_improvement}
\alpha_{\mathrm{EI}}(\boldsymbol{\lambda})
= \mathbb{E}\big[ \max(0,\, \mathcal{J}_{\mathrm{best}} - \mathcal{J}(\boldsymbol{\lambda}) ) \big],
\end{equation}
which has the closed-form expression:
\begin{equation}
\label{eq:expected_improvement_closed_form}
\alpha_{\mathrm{EI}}(\boldsymbol{\lambda}) 
= (\mathcal{J}_{\mathrm{best}} - \mu_{t}(\boldsymbol{\lambda})) 
\Phi(Z) + \sigma_{t}(\boldsymbol{\lambda}) \phi(Z),
\qquad
Z = \frac{\mathcal{J}_{\mathrm{best}} - \mu_{t}(\boldsymbol{\lambda})}{\sigma_{t}(\boldsymbol{\lambda})},
\end{equation}
where $\Phi$ and $\phi$ denote the standard normal CDF and PDF respectively.

This acquisition function assigns high values to regions where:
\begin{itemize}
    \item the GP predicts low objective values (\textit{exploitation}), or
    \item the model uncertainty $\sigma_{t}$ is high (\textit{exploration}).
\end{itemize}

\paragraph{Sequential Optimization Loop.}
At each iteration, the Bayesian optimization loop performs:
\begin{enumerate}
    \item Update the GP surrogate using all observed evaluations.
    \item Select the next hyperparameter configuration:
    \begin{equation}
    \label{eq:next_hyperparameter}
    \boldsymbol{\lambda}_{t+1}
    = \arg\max_{\boldsymbol{\lambda} \in \Lambda}
    \alpha(\boldsymbol{\lambda}; \mathcal{D}_{t}).
    \end{equation}
    \item Train the model under configuration $\boldsymbol{\lambda}_{t+1}$ and compute its validation score:
    \begin{equation}
    \label{eq:validation_score}
    y_{t+1} = \mathcal{J}(\boldsymbol{\lambda}_{t+1}).
    \end{equation}
    \item Expand the dataset:
    \begin{equation}
    \label{eq:dataset_expansion}
    \mathcal{D}_{t+1} = \mathcal{D}_{t} \cup \{ (\boldsymbol{\lambda}_{t+1}, y_{t+1}) \}.
    \end{equation}
\end{enumerate}

The process continues until convergence or until a fixed evaluation budget is exhausted.
The final estimate of the optimal hyperparameters is:
\begin{equation}
\label{eq:optimal_hyperparameters}
\boldsymbol{\lambda}^{\star}
= \arg\min_{(\boldsymbol{\lambda}_{i},\, y_{i}) \in \mathcal{D}_{T}}
y_{i}.
\end{equation}

\paragraph{Grounding in our framework.}
In practice, the set of hyperparameters $\boldsymbol{\lambda}$ that defines each architecture is precisely what this procedure optimizes, and the search is run independently for every model (the inverse estimator and each forward generator). Bayesian optimization is thus the element that takes the hyperparameter configuration generating a given architecture and steers it toward its best attainable performance --- for instance, maximizing parameter-recovery accuracy for the inverse network, and reconstruction fidelity (SSIM and $L_1$) under a non-collapsed latent code for the cVAE, or sample quality for the diffusion model. The optimized blocks are then composed into the cycle-consistent framework of Section~\ref{sec:cycle_consistency}, so that the closed loop operates with each component already at its optimum.

\subsection{Specific Objective 2: Exploiting State Transitions for Degeneracy Resolution}\label{sec:temporal_objective}

This subsection addresses the second specific objective, which complements the cycle-consistent framework of Section~\ref{sec:Material_methods} where it is only partially effective. As argued there, when distinct parameter sets relax into genuinely indistinguishable single-axis ($s_z$) equilibrium textures, no image-based constraint acting on a \emph{single} static state can recover the missing information. The strategy proposed here supplies that information by moving from a single equilibrium image to the \emph{ordered sequence of states} the system visits as an ambient control (temperature or applied field) is varied---an idea still under active development, of which we sketch here the governing principle and its intended realization.

\paragraph{Governing principle: different interactions, different transitions.}
The starting observation is physical: although two parameter sets $\boldsymbol{\theta}$ and $\boldsymbol{\theta}'$ may produce nearly identical textures at one operating point, the \emph{way} those textures evolve as the ambient conditions change is governed by the specific balance of interactions and is therefore generally different. A chiral (DMI-dominated) state and an exchange-frustrated state may look alike at a given temperature, yet they nucleate, coarsen, and disorder along distinct thermal paths. Formally, let $c_1 < c_2 < \dots < c_K$ denote a schedule of the ambient control and let
\begin{equation}\label{eq:state_sequence}
\mathbf{X}_{1:K}(\boldsymbol{\theta}) = \big(\mathbf{X}^{(1)}(\boldsymbol{\theta}),\,\mathbf{X}^{(2)}(\boldsymbol{\theta}),\,\dots,\,\mathbf{X}^{(K)}(\boldsymbol{\theta})\big)
\end{equation}
be the corresponding ordered sequence of magnetization images---in effect a short ``video'' of the transition. Figure~\ref{fig:temporal_series} shows a representative such sequence along a thermal schedule.

\begin{figure}[H]
    \centering
    \includegraphics[width=\linewidth]{Figures/convergence}
    \caption{A representative sequence of states $\mathbf{X}_{1:K}(\boldsymbol{\theta})$ along a thermal schedule (the annealing trajectory): the main curve is the total energy $\mathcal{H}$ versus the exchange-normalized temperature $T^{(0)}$, and each inset is the $s_z$ image at that thermal state, from the disordered paramagnet at high $T^{(0)}$ to the relaxed texture at low $T^{(0)}$. The \emph{ordered evolution} of these images---not merely the final frame---is what encodes the underlying couplings.}
    \label{fig:temporal_series}
\end{figure}

\paragraph{Information-theoretic rationale.}
The sequence is strictly more informative about the parameters than any single frame. Writing $I(\,\cdot\,;\,\cdot\,)$ for mutual information and $H(\,\cdot\mid\cdot\,)$ for conditional (posterior) entropy, the trajectory satisfies
\begin{equation}\label{eq:mi_inequality}
I\big(\boldsymbol{\theta};\,\mathbf{X}_{1:K}\big) \;\ge\; \max_{k}\, I\big(\boldsymbol{\theta};\,\mathbf{X}^{(k)}\big),
\qquad\text{equivalently}\qquad
H\big(\boldsymbol{\theta}\mid \mathbf{X}_{1:K}\big) \;\le\; H\big(\boldsymbol{\theta}\mid \mathbf{X}^{(k)}\big),
\end{equation}
so that conditioning on the whole sequence can only sharpen the posterior over $\boldsymbol{\theta}$. The residual, image-degenerate cases of Objective~1 correspond precisely to configurations where a single frame carries little parameter information ($I(\boldsymbol{\theta};\mathbf{X}^{(k)})$ small) while the transition does not ($I(\boldsymbol{\theta};\mathbf{X}_{1:K})$ appreciably larger); it is this information gap that the temporal strategy is designed to exploit.

\paragraph{Intended realization.}
The concrete methodology, still to be finalized, follows diffusion-based generative philosophies extended along the temporal axis: rather than generating (or conditioning on) a single image, the model treats $\mathbf{X}_{1:K}$ jointly---in the spirit of sequence/video diffusion---so that the denoiser learns the \emph{dynamics} of the transition conditioned on $\boldsymbol{\theta}$, and an information-theoretic term encourages sequences whose evolution is maximally discriminative of the parameters (i.e., that maximize the gap in Equation~\ref{eq:mi_inequality}). Coupled back into the inverse estimator, this turns ``how a texture transitions'' into an additional, physically grounded signal for resolving the degeneracy that equilibrium images alone cannot. The precise architecture, schedule design, and information estimator are open questions reserved for the development of this objective.

\subsection{Specific Objective 3: Sim-to-Real Domain Adaptation}

This subsection addresses the third specific objective: bridging the gap between synthetic micromagnetic simulations and experimental imaging so that the forward and inverse blocks of Objective~1 remain reliable when confronted with real measurements. As established in Section~\ref{sec:sim2real}, the systematic distribution shift between idealized simulations and experimental observations---noise, instrumental artifacts, finite spatial resolution, and physical imperfections---is a fundamental barrier to deployment. We emphasize from the outset that this objective is an \emph{active research direction still being scoped}: rather than committing to a single mechanism, we are currently \emph{evaluating} several candidate strategies, a decision informed by review feedback cautioning that a purely statistical, black-box alignment is neither the most rigorous nor the most physically grounded option.

{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\subsubsection{Design Preference: Preprocessing over Extra Losses or Models}

A deliberate design choice, consistent with the compact-objective philosophy adopted in Objective~1 (Section~\ref{sec:cycle_consistency}), is to favor \emph{preprocessing}-based adaptation over approaches that would enlarge the training objective or add auxiliary trained networks. Concretely, the preferred route is to transform the data itself---mapping experimental images toward the synthetic domain on which the estimator was trained, or conversely enriching the synthetic data toward the experimental one---as a stage that sits \emph{before} the already-trained inverse estimator $f_{\hat{\Theta}}$, leaving its weights and its loss untouched.

This choice directly answers a reviewer concern regarding methodological complexity: it avoids introducing an additional domain-alignment loss term (with its extra weighting hyperparameter and potential gradient conflict) or an adversarial sub-model, both of which would complicate training without a clear physical justification. Because adaptation is confined to a preprocessing stage, the pipeline remains modular, the closed-loop consistency of Section~\ref{sec:cycle_consistency} is preserved unchanged, and each component can be validated in isolation. As this objective is still being refined, we make the current stance explicit: we \emph{discard}, for now, any approach that would introduce an intermediate trained model to align the latent spaces of the simulated and experimental data---as such a component would enlarge and entangle the current cost functions---and instead confine the strategy to \emph{data-level} preprocessing of the experimental (or synthetic) images that brings the two domains closer upstream of the already-trained estimator, with the specific operator to be selected empirically. Consistent with the recommendation to move beyond purely statistical alignment toward new metrics, the criterion for closing the domain gap is itself being reoriented toward \emph{physical} descriptors: rather than matching black-box feature statistics, we are evaluating whether physically meaningful observables---in particular the spin--spin correlation function and related quantities---are \emph{conserved} between the two domains, so that domain proximity is measured by the preservation of physical properties across images. This line remains under active investigation.

\subsubsection{Candidate Strategies Under Evaluation}

Building on the domain-adaptation survey of Section~\ref{sec:domain_adaptation_sota}, the options currently being weighed---beyond the conventional statistical feature alignment (e.g., MMD or CORAL) that the review flagged as insufficient on its own---are the more physics-aware alternatives:
\begin{itemize}
    \item[--] \textbf{Physics-based forward modeling.} Rendering the synthetic magnetic states through a model of the instrument's observation operator (projection geometry, point-spread function, detector noise) so that simulated and experimental images live in the same \emph{observable} space, directly attacking the observable gap of Section~\ref{sec:sim2real}.
    \item[--] \textbf{Domain randomization.} Broadly randomizing the simulator's noise, contrast, and resolution during data generation so that the experimental domain becomes one sample within a wide synthetic distribution, enabling label-free transfer.
    \item[--] \textbf{Physics-guided image-to-image translation.} A preprocessing translator between the two image manifolds, constrained to preserve physically meaningful content (domain-wall topology, characteristic wavevector) rather than arbitrary pixel appearance.
    \item[--] \textbf{Contrastive self-supervised alignment.} Learning domain-invariant representations from unlabeled data of both domains, so that the estimator sees comparable features regardless of origin.
\end{itemize}

The choice among these will be made empirically against the experimental reference datasets described in Section~\ref{sec:experimental_datasets}---the Leeds Skyrmion (STM), Co/Pd (MFM), and KIT 4D-STEM magnetic-field collections---which already provide realistic imaging signatures (measurement noise, artifacts, finite resolution) absent from simulation. Because these experimental data are already available as a validation target, the evaluation of the candidate strategies is grounded rather than hypothetical.}

\subsubsection{Uncertainty at Deployment}

Because experimental images may remain compatible with several parameter configurations---owing to the degeneracy discussed in Objective~1, measurement noise, and the residual sim-to-real gap---the inverse estimator will, when applied to real data, report parameter \emph{intervals} rather than deterministic point estimates. Lightweight mechanisms such as Monte Carlo dropout or deep ensembles are being considered for this purpose, as they add no separate model and integrate directly with the existing estimator. As with the adaptation strategy itself, the specific uncertainty mechanism is a design choice to be finalized during this objective.

\jc{\paragraph{Quantitative validation on experimental images without ground-truth labels.} A key examiner concern is that public MFM/STM images carry no ground-truth Hamiltonian parameters, so a naive validation would remain purely qualitative and could not distinguish genuine parametric accuracy from mere visual similarity under degeneracy. We therefore adopt a \emph{label-free, physically grounded} validation protocol that closes the loop of Objective~1 on real data: given an experimental image $\mathbf{X}_{\mathrm{exp}}$, the inverse estimator produces $\hat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}$, the forward generator regenerates $\hat{\mathbf{X}}=g_{\Phi}(\hat{\boldsymbol{\theta}})$, and agreement is quantified not in pixel space but through the same rotation- and translation-invariant physical observables used throughout this work---magnetization $M$ and $|M|$, the spin--spin correlation function $C(r)$, and the structure factor $S(k)$ with its peak wavevector $k^{\ast}$. Because these descriptors are computed directly on both $\mathbf{X}_{\mathrm{exp}}$ and $\hat{\mathbf{X}}$, they provide a scalar, quantitative discrepancy that needs \emph{no} knowledge of the true parameters, turning the closed loop into an automatic consistency test on experimental data. This physical-observable agreement, combined with the uncertainty intervals above, replaces subjective visual inspection with a reproducible quantitative criterion, and directly guards against the failure mode the examiner identified---visual resemblance mistaken for parametric precision.}

We stress that this objective remains under active development: the exact preprocessing operator, the extent of physics-based forward modeling, and the uncertainty mechanism are open questions, and the final methodology will be selected once the candidate strategies above have been evaluated on the available experimental data.

\subsection{Specific Objective 4: Interpretability and Physical Validation}

This subsection addresses the third specific objective: implementing interpretability mechanisms that enable physical validation and traceability of model predictions. The methodology integrates multiple complementary techniques—post-hoc attribution, regression activation maps, latent space visualization, and physics-guided structural constraints—to ensure that learned representations align with underlying micromagnetic principles.

A central requirement of the proposed forward--inverse framework is the ability to interpret how the architectures --- both the forward generator $g_{\Phi}$ and the inverse estimator $f_{\hat{\Theta}}$ --- extract and use the physical information encoded in the magnetization images, and to verify that this behavior is preserved across the simulated and experimental domains.

{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\paragraph{Why post-hoc interpretability for this problem.}
Consistent with the analysis of Section~\ref{sec:state_art_interp}, we deliberately adopt \emph{post-hoc} attribution rather than an intrinsic, physics-hardwired interpretable architecture. The reason is specific to the structure of our problem. First, the task is a high-dimensional \emph{multi-output regression}: many continuous Hamiltonian parameters must be probed jointly, several of them weakly identifiable, so the interpretability machinery needs enough \emph{degrees of freedom} to be applied uniformly per parameter, per layer, and per phase, rather than fixing a single physical decomposition at design time. Second, post-hoc methods operate on an \emph{already-trained} model in essentially one backward pass, so they can be reused across heterogeneous backbones (the convolutional and Vision-Transformer regressors compared in Objective~1) without retraining—an efficiency argument that matters for the intended agent-oriented integration of Objective~5, where interpretability is invoked as a reusable capability over any trained predictor (Section~\ref{sec:state_art_agents}). For these reasons we build the interpretability layer around a regression-tailored attribution map (RAMs) and benchmark it against the standard perturbation-based explainers (LIME, SHAP, and its tractable Kernel~SHAP estimator), complementing both with a latent-space manifold analysis (UMAP and PHATE).

Concretely, we combine a spatially resolved, regression-oriented attribution method (RAMs), which quantifies \emph{where} in the image each Hamiltonian parameter is inferred from, with a global latent-space analysis (UMAP and PHATE) that exposes the structure of the learned representations. The perturbation-based explainers (LIME, SHAP, Kernel~SHAP) and standard baselines (Integrated Gradients and occlusion analysis) are used as quantitative and qualitative points of comparison.}

\subsubsection{Spatial Interpretability via RAMs}\label{sec:rams}

Interpretability methods based on activation mapping have become a foundational approach for understanding the decision-making processes of deep neural networks. By identifying the spatial regions within an input that most significantly influence a model's prediction, these methods project opaque latent representations back into a human-interpretable domain. Among these, Gradient-weighted Class Activation Mapping (Grad-CAM) and its variants have been extensively utilized in computer vision to generate class-specific saliency maps~\cite{vinogradova2020gradcam}. 

In standard classification paradigms, given an input image $\mathbf{X}$ and a discrete target class score $y^c$, activation maps are computed by extracting the gradients of $y^c$ with respect to the $k$-th feature map $\mathbf{A}^k \in \mathbb{R}^{H' \times W'}$ of a terminal convolutional layer (where $H'$ and $W'$ denote the spatial dimensions of the feature map). The spatial importance weights are derived by globally pooling these gradients, and the final map is a ReLU-activated linear combination of the feature maps~\cite{zhang2024gradcam}. While highly effective for discrete semantic categorization, this standard formulation structurally fails in continuous regression tasks. The absence of a discrete target class removes the natural selection mechanism required to isolate the output dimension driving the interpretability process. Furthermore, directly computing gradients with respect to a prediction $\hat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}$ introduces severe gradient instability; minute fluctuations in the scalar output can yield drastically different spatial attributions, failing to provide the physical consistency required for analyzing complex Hamiltonian parameters~\cite{makke2024symbolic}.

\paragraph{RAMs for CNN-based Approaches.}\label{sec:CNN_RAMs}

To overcome the fundamental limitations of discrete activation methods in highly degenerate physical systems, we introduce RAMs, a novel formulation explicitly tailored to bridge continuous parameter estimation with spatial topologies. Given an input magnetic domain visualization $\mathbf{X}$ and the optimized regression model $f_{\hat{\Theta}} : \mathbb{R}^{\breve{H} \times \breve{W}} \rightarrow \mathbb{R}^{\breve{P}}$, the network predicts a vector of physical constants $\hat{\boldsymbol{\theta}} = f_{\hat{\Theta}}(\mathbf{X})$. Let $\hat{\theta}_p \in \hat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}$ denote the continuous prediction for the $p$-th target Hamiltonian parameter (e.g., the DMI strength $\tilde{D}$ or symmetric exchange $\tilde{J}$), and let $\theta_p \in \boldsymbol{\theta}$ represents its corresponding ground truth. Instead of utilizing a raw predicted scalar, we formulate a scale-invariant, error-based activation objective $\mathcal{O}_p$. To prevent the mathematical discontinuities and negative objective bounds inherent to absolute error formulations ($1 - |\theta_p - \hat{\theta}_p|$), we utilize a normalized Gaussian decay function:

\begin{equation}\label{eq:RAM_Objective}
\mathcal{O}_p = \exp\left(-\gamma_p \left(\theta_p - \hat{\theta}_p\right)^2\right),
\end{equation}
where $\gamma_p>0$ is a hyperparameter-specific precision factor determining the strictness of the error decay. This objective smoothly bounds the activation target strictly within $(0, 1]$, promoting maximum gradient response precisely when the network's prediction asymptotically approaches the true thermodynamic value, thereby isolating the topological features that positively contribute to accurate regression.

The spatial importance weights $\alpha_k^{(p)}$ for each continuous feature map $\mathbf{A}^k$ are subsequently computed by globally averaging the gradients of the error objective $\mathcal{O}_p$ with respect to the local spatial activations $\mathbf{A}_{ij}^k$:

\begin{equation}\label{eq:RAM_Weights}
\alpha_k^{(p)} = \frac{1}{H' W'} \sum_{i=1}^{H'} \sum_{j=1}^{W'} \frac{\partial \mathcal{O}_p}{\partial \mathbf{A}_{ij}^k}.
\end{equation}

These weights strictly quantify the importance of the $k$-th latent spatial feature in successfully minimizing the prediction error for the target physical parameter $\theta_p$. The resulting RAM is then formulated as:

\begin{equation}\label{eq:RAM_Formulation}
\mathbf{\mathcal{R}}_{\text{RAM}}^{(p)} = \text{ReLU} \left( \sum_k \alpha_k^{(p)} \mathbf{A}^k \right).
\end{equation}

The application of the ReLU activation ensures that only spatial features exerting a positive, stabilizing influence on the estimation of the target parameter are preserved. Finally, $\mathcal{R}_{\text{RAM}}^{(p)}$ is spatially upsampled to the original input resolution $\breve{H} \times \breve{W}$ and normalized to the interval $[0,1]$. Then, by mapping the continuous error gradients back to the input spatial magnetization field $\mathbf{X}$, RAMs allow for the direct visual and quantitative identification of the specific emergent textures that the network utilizes to deduce the underlying energetic balances. The complete workflow for this localized spatial interpretability is illustrated in Figure~\ref{fig:rams_pipeline}.


%RAMs
\begin{figure}[H]
  \centering
  \vspace{0.3cm}
  \resizebox{0.65\linewidth}{!}{
  \input{Figures/RAMs}
  }
  \caption{RAMs extraction pipeline. Latent feature maps and continuous predictions from the network backbone are explicitly coupled via an error-decay objective. Gradient-based importance weights combine these features to produce a normalized, physically traceable spatial saliency map for the target Hamiltonian parameter $\theta_{p}$.}
  \label{fig:rams_pipeline}
\end{figure}


\paragraph{RAMs for ViT-based Approaches.}\label{sec:ViT_RAMs}
%ViT RAMs
While our standard RAMs formulation seamlessly integrates with CNNs by exploiting their innate spatial hierarchies, extending this interpretability metric to the Vision Transformers requires mapping sequence-based latent representations back to the two-dimensional physical domain. Unlike CNNs, which preserve spatial locality through sliding convolutional filters, the ViT processes the input spatial magnetization field $\mathbf{X}$ as a flattened sequence of $N_r$ non-overlapping patches, outputting a discrete sequence of latent embeddings. To evaluate ViT-based architectures within the exact same RAMs framework, we introduce a token-reshaping mechanism that bridges the one-dimensional transformer latent space with the two-dimensional topological geometry. Let the output of the terminal Transformer encoder block be denoted as $\mathbf{Z} \in \mathbb{R}^{(N_r + 1) \times D}$, where $D$ is the embedding dimension and the extra token corresponds to the global aggregation token (e.g., the \texttt{[CLS]} token) utilized by the regression head to predict the Hamiltonian parameters $\hat{\mathbf{y}}$. By isolating the $N_r$ spatial tokens, we obtain the localized sequence $\mathbf{Z}_s \in \mathbb{R}^{N_r \times D}$. Because each sequence token explicitly corresponds to a fixed physical patch of the original nanodot domain, $\mathbf{Z}_s$ can be folded back into a 2D spatial grid. Assuming square patches, the sequence is reshaped into a pseudo-feature map:

\begin{equation}\label{eq:ViT_Reshape}
\mathbf{A}_{\text{ViT}} = \text{Reshape}(\mathbf{Z}_s) \in \mathbb{R}^{\sqrt{N_r} \times \sqrt{N_r} \times D}.
\end{equation}

Once projected into this tensor format, $\mathbf{A}_{\text{ViT}}$ functions structurally identically to the terminal convolutional feature map $\mathbf{A}$ utilized in the CNN baseline. The $k$-th slice of the embedding dimension ($k \in \{1, \dots, D\}$) forms the latent spatial map $\mathbf{A}_{\text{ViT}}^k \in \mathbb{R}^{\sqrt{N_r} \times \sqrt{N_r}}$. We then apply the identical scale-invariant, error-based activation objective $\mathcal{O}_p$ defined in Equation~\ref{eq:RAM_Objective}. The spatial importance weights for the ViT embeddings are computed by globally averaging the gradients of this objective with respect to the reshaped spatial tokens:

\begin{equation}\label{eq:ViT_Weights}
\alpha_k^{(p)} = \frac{1}{N_r} \sum_{i=1}^{\sqrt{N_r}} \sum_{j=1}^{\sqrt{N_r}} \frac{\partial \mathcal{O}_p}{\partial (\mathbf{A}_{\text{ViT}})_{ij}^k}.
\end{equation}

RAMs for the ViT are constructed via the ReLU-activated linear combination of these reshaped embedding slices, precisely mirroring Equation~\ref{eq:RAM_Formulation}, before being upsampled to the original input resolution $\breve{H} \times \breve{W}$. This extension guarantees an architecture-agnostic evaluation of interpretability, ensuring that the isolated degenerate textures driving the model's predictions can be fairly compared across fundamentally disparate deep learning architectures.

\paragraph{Normalized RAMs for Layer-Wise and Target-Specific Analysis.}\label{sec:Nor_RAMs}

To rigorously compare the spatial activations across different Hamiltonian parameters and network depths, it is imperative to compute the RAMs utilizing strictly normalized predictions and targets. Let $\bar{\theta}_p \in [0,1]$ and $\hat{\bar{\theta}}_p \in [0,1]$ denote the Min-Max normalized ground truth and predicted values for the $p$-th physical parameter, respectively. By scaling the variables to a uniform interval, the layer-specific error objective is strictly bounded and evaluated as $\mathcal{O}_p = \exp(-\gamma_p (\bar{\theta}_p - \hat{\bar{\theta}}_p)^2)$. For a given convolutional or transformer layer $l \in \{1, \dots, L\}$, we extract the layer-specific unnormalized RAM, denoted as $\mathcal{R}_{\text{RAM}}^{(l,p)}$, following the gradient and activation protocols established in Equations~\ref{eq:RAM_Weights} and \ref{eq:RAM_Formulation}. To quantitatively evaluate and isolate the relative contribution of each distinct layer to the estimation of every physical constant, we introduce a global cross-layer and cross-parameter normalization scheme. The globally normalized activation map, $\tilde{\mathcal{R}}_{\text{RAM}}^{(l,p)}\in[0,1]$, is defined by scaling the layer-specific maps against the absolute maximum spatial activation observed across all $L$ layers and all $\breve{P}$ parameters:

\begin{equation}\label{eq:RAM_Global_Norm}
\tilde{\mathcal{R}}_{\text{RAM}}^{(l,p)} = \frac{\mathcal{R}_{\text{RAM}}^{(l,p)}}{\max\limits_{l' \in \{1,\dots,L\}, p' \in \{1,\dots,\breve{P}\}} \left( \max\limits_{i,j} \left[ \mathcal{R}_{\text{RAM}}^{(l',p')}\right]_{i,j} \right)}.
\end{equation}

This comprehensive normalization ensures that the gradient magnitudes are dimensionally consistent and comparable, preventing parameters with intrinsically higher variance or layers with larger gradient flows from disproportionately dominating the interpretability metrics. By tracking the evolution of $\tilde{\mathcal{R}}_{\text{RAM}}^{(l,p)}$ across the network depth, this formulation explicitly reveals how the deep learning architecture bypasses the intrinsically ill-conditioned nature of the inverse problem. In magnetic nanodots, severe structural ambiguity arises because disparate energetic combinations of the symmetric exchange ($\tilde{J}$), DMI ($\tilde{D}$), magnetocrystalline anisotropy ($\tilde{K}$), and Zeeman field ($\tilde{H}$) can yield highly degenerate states with nearly identical free energies. Then, the global normalization aim to code how the network dynamically resolves this degeneracy through a hierarchical physical decoupling. We establish that early network layers (lower $l$) exhibit high relative activations for parameters governing short-range spin interactions, capturing the localized chiral winding angles and domain wall boundaries dictated primarily by the delicate competition between $\tilde{J}$ and $\tilde{D}$. Conversely, deeper layers (higher $l$) aggregate these localized topological textures to resolve macro-scale thermodynamic stability. These terminal layers exhibit dominant spatial activations for $\tilde{H}$ and $\tilde{K}$, which govern the global topological confinement, phase transitions, and spatial alignment of emergent structures such as skyrmion cores and labyrinthine patterns. By correlating the intensity and spatial localization of $\tilde{\mathcal{R}}_{\text{RAM}}^{(l,p)}$ across the network hierarchy, our framework utilizes both localized boundary chirality and global domain spacing to disambiguate the underlying Hamiltonian parameters.

\subsubsection{Benchmarking RAMs against Perturbation-Based Attribution}\label{sec:rams_benchmark}

To position RAMs within the broader family of post-hoc explainers, we benchmark them against the three model-agnostic attribution methods most widely used in the literature (Section~\ref{sec:state_art_interp}), adapted here to the continuous regression setting: LIME~\cite{ribeiro2016should}, SHAP~\cite{lundberg2017unified}, and its tractable Kernel~SHAP estimator~\cite{lundberg2017unified}. For a target parameter $p$, let $f_p:\mathbb{R}^{\breve H\times\breve W}\!\rightarrow\mathbb{R}$ return the $p$-th predicted parameter, $f_p(\mathbf{X})=\hat\theta_p$. A key practical consequence of working with \emph{images} rather than tabular features is that these methods cannot perturb individual pixels---doing so violates the exchange-driven spatial continuity of the magnetization field (Section~\ref{sec:state_art_interp}). We therefore operate them at the \emph{superpixel} level: the input is partitioned into $d$ disjoint interpretable regions $\{\mathcal{X}_1,\dots,\mathcal{X}_d\}$ obtained by spatial segmentation, and a coalition vector $\mathbf{z}\in\{0,1\}^d$ encodes which regions are retained ($z_i=1$) or masked to a reference baseline ($z_i=0$), with $h_{\mathbf{X}}(\mathbf{z})$ reconstructing the perturbed image. The segmentation granularity is fixed on a physical basis, choosing the number of segments so that each superpixel matches the characteristic magnetic-domain width rather than an arbitrary pixel cluster.

Within this shared superpixel encoding, \emph{LIME} fits a sparse local linear surrogate $g(\mathbf{z})=\phi_0+\sum_i \phi_i z_i$ around $\mathbf{X}$ by minimizing a proximity-weighted least-squares objective, while \emph{SHAP} attributes to each region its average marginal contribution across coalitions (the Shapley value), whose exact evaluation requires $2^{d}$ model queries and is therefore approximated by \emph{Kernel~SHAP} through a weighted linear regression over sampled coalitions. The decisive contrast with RAMs is one of \emph{cost and layer-level resolution}: being gradient-based, a RAM requires a single backward pass and can be computed at \emph{any} network layer, exposing the depth-wise hierarchy of Equation~\ref{eq:RAM_Global_Norm}; the perturbation baselines instead require of order $10^3$ masked model evaluations per map and yield a single input-level attribution with no access to the internal, layer-resolved decomposition. We will report both faithfulness (via a deletion protocol on the most-relevant regions) and wall-clock cost, and we leave the incorporation of additional complementary mechanisms (e.g., frequency-resolved Shapley estimators or physics-aware perturbations) open for future evaluation.

\subsubsection{Latent-Space Analysis via UMAP and PHATE}\label{sec:umap}

{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}To examine the global structure of the internal representations, we project the high-dimensional latent features extracted from both the forward and inverse models into two dimensions. Rather than adopting a single embedding method as an arbitrary default, we motivate the choice explicitly and use \emph{two complementary} nonlinear manifold-learning techniques whose inductive biases are matched to the two questions we ask of the latent space: static cluster geometry (UMAP) and continuous transition structure (PHATE).

\paragraph{Justification and formulation of UMAP.}
Uniform Manifold Approximation and Projection (UMAP)~\cite{ghojogh2021uniform} is selected---over linear projections (PCA), which cannot unfold curved manifolds, and over t-SNE, which preserves local neighborhoods but distorts global inter-cluster geometry---because it is grounded in a fuzzy-topological representation that preserves \emph{both} local and moderate global structure while scaling to our sample sizes. UMAP first builds a weighted $k$-nearest-neighbor graph in which the directed similarity between latent vectors $\mathbf{h}_i,\mathbf{h}_j$ is a locally adaptive exponential kernel,
\begin{equation}\label{eq:umap_highdim}
p_{j|i}=\exp\!\left(-\frac{\max\big(0,\,d(\mathbf{h}_i,\mathbf{h}_j)-\rho_i\big)}{\sigma_i}\right),\qquad
p_{ij}=p_{j|i}+p_{i|j}-p_{j|i}\,p_{i|j},
\end{equation}
where $\rho_i$ is the distance to the nearest neighbor and $\sigma_i$ is calibrated so that each point has a fixed effective connectivity. In the two-dimensional embedding $\{\mathbf{y}_i\}$ the similarities follow a heavy-tailed kernel $q_{ij}=\big(1+a\lVert\mathbf{y}_i-\mathbf{y}_j\rVert^{2b}\big)^{-1}$, and the layout is found by minimizing the fuzzy cross-entropy $\sum_{ij}\big[p_{ij}\log\tfrac{p_{ij}}{q_{ij}}+(1-p_{ij})\log\tfrac{1-p_{ij}}{1-q_{ij}}\big]$. Applied to our models, this yields:
\begin{itemize}
    \item[--] In the \emph{forward model}, a test of whether variations in the latent code $\mathbf{z}$ vary smoothly with the physical parameter vector $\boldsymbol{\theta}$.
    \item[--] In the \emph{inverse model}, a visualization of how magnetic textures with similar $\boldsymbol{\theta}$ organize in the encoder feature space, revealing the magnetic-phase clusters (ferromagnetic, helical, labyrinthine, and skyrmionic) employed in the closed-loop evaluation of Section~\ref{sec:cycle_consistency}.
\end{itemize}

\paragraph{PHATE for transition-aware structure.}
UMAP is optimized for cluster separation and can fragment a genuine \emph{continuum} into artificially discrete islands. Because a central goal of this thesis is to analyze the \emph{transient series of magnetic states} generated as ambient conditions are varied (Objective~2, Section~\ref{sec:temporal_objective})---where the physically meaningful signal is precisely the continuity of the transition between phases---we additionally evaluate PHATE (Potential of Heat-diffusion for Affinity-based Trajectory Embedding)~\cite{moon2019phate}, a method designed to preserve continual progressions and branches rather than only clusters. PHATE builds a local affinity kernel between latent points, row-normalizes it into a Markov diffusion operator $\mathbf{P}$, and raises it to a power $t$ so that $\mathbf{P}^{t}$ integrates transition probabilities over multiple scales; an information-geometric \emph{potential distance}
\begin{equation}\label{eq:phate_potential}
\mathcal{D}_{ij}^{(t)}=\big\lVert\, -\log \mathbf{P}^{t}_{i,:}\;+\;\log \mathbf{P}^{t}_{j,:}\,\big\rVert_2
\end{equation}
is then embedded into two dimensions by metric multidimensional scaling. Powering the diffusion operator makes PHATE emphasize the global trajectory geometry, so that a smooth sweep of $\boldsymbol{\theta}$ appears as a continuous path rather than disconnected blobs---exactly the representation needed to test the ``different interactions, different transitions'' hypothesis of Objective~2.

Together, these embeddings expose global patterns such as parameter identifiability, the degeneracy of magnetization textures, and the geometry of the learned manifolds (Figure~\ref{fig:interpretability_strategy}): well-structured UMAP clusters indicate that the encoder and generator learn representations aligned with the underlying physical regimes, while the PHATE trajectories test whether the temporal state sequences of Objective~2 trace coherent, physically ordered transition paths.}
