\subsection{Specific Objective 5: Integration into an Agent-Orientable Tool Package}\label{sec:agent_integration}

The final specific objective is integrative rather than a new model: it turns the capabilities developed for Objectives~1--4---GPU-accelerated simulation, forward and inverse mapping, temporal transition analysis, sim-to-real preprocessing, and post-hoc interpretability---from a loose collection of expert-operated scripts into a single, coherent, and reusable system. As motivated in the problem statement (Section~\ref{sec:problem_statement}) and surveyed in Section~\ref{sec:state_art_agents}, the value here is not another architecture but a shared, physics-grounded substrate through which each capability can be discovered, invoked, and \emph{chained} in a controlled, auditable way, including by autonomous AI agents.

\subsubsection{From Scripts to Composable Skills}

We will encapsulate each physical capability as a self-contained \emph{tool} with a typed, documented contract: an explicit input/output schema, unit and shape conventions consistent with the notation of Sections~\ref{sec:cycle_consistency}--\ref{sec:umap}, and a deterministic, versioned behavior. A thin \emph{skill} layer then composes these tools into higher-level operations that mirror the scientific workflow. Crucially, this is designed to avoid the ``ensemble of disconnected tools'' pattern: all tools share the same Hamiltonian parameter vector $\boldsymbol{\theta}$, the same $s_z$ image representation, and the same physical-observable definitions, so that the output of one is a valid, physically consistent input to the next. The intended tool catalog, mapped to the preceding objectives, is:
\begin{itemize}
    \item[--] \textbf{Simulator} (dataset generation): given $\boldsymbol{\theta}$, produce equilibrium $s_z$ configurations and, optionally, the transition trajectory $\mathbf{X}_{1:K}(\boldsymbol{\theta})$, using the GPU-accelerated tensor engine of Section~\ref{sec:cycle_consistency}.
    \item[--] \textbf{Estimator} (inverse map): given an $s_z$ image, return $\hat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}$ with per-parameter uncertainty intervals (Objectives~1 and~3).
    \item[--] \textbf{Generator} (forward map): given $\boldsymbol{\theta}$, synthesize a magnetization image via the selected conditional generative model (diffusion or cVAE) of Objective~1.
    \item[--] \textbf{Interpreter}: given a trained model and an image, return RAMs and UMAP/PHATE latent-space summaries (Objective~4).
    \item[--] \textbf{Domain-adapter}: a preprocessing operator that maps an experimental image toward the synthetic domain before estimation (Objective~3, under evaluation).
\end{itemize}

\subsubsection{Standardized Interface and Agent Compatibility}

To make these tools reusable beyond bespoke glue code, they will be exposed through a standardized, protocol-based interface. We target the Model Context Protocol (MCP)~\cite{hou2025mcp} as the concrete mechanism, since it exposes tools through a uniform, auditable client--server contract that external orchestrators---including LLM-based scientific agents~\cite{gridach2025agentic,ghafarollahi2024atomagents}---can query and invoke without a custom connector per tool. Under this interface, a canonical workflow such as
\begin{equation}\label{eq:agent_chain}
\text{simulate}\;(\boldsymbol{\theta})\;\rightarrow\;\text{estimate}\;(\hat{\boldsymbol{\theta}})\;\rightarrow\;\text{interpret}\;\rightarrow\;\text{regenerate}\;(\hat{\mathbf{X}})
\end{equation}
becomes a sequence of discoverable calls rather than a hand-wired pipeline, so that reproducing a result or composing a new analysis no longer requires tacit expertise. The closed-loop consistency of Objective~1 provides a natural self-check within this chain: an agent can regenerate $\hat{\mathbf{X}}=g_{\Phi}(\hat{\boldsymbol{\theta}})$ from an estimate and compare it to the input, obtaining an automatic, physically grounded confidence signal.

We emphasize that this objective is the capstone that binds the direct problem, the inverse problem, their temporal extension, and their interpretation into one physically grounded, reusable workflow; the specific packaging conventions and the extent of the exposed skill set will be refined as the individual capabilities of Objectives~1--4 are consolidated. The role of this integration layer within the overall methodology is shown as Objective~5 in the framework of Figure~\ref{fig:proposed_pipeline}.

\section{Experimental Set Up} \label{sec:Exp}

\subsection{Computational Infrastructure}

All experiments reported in this work were conducted in a cloud-based environment on RunPod, under a consistent computational and experiment-tracking setup applied across all architectures.

\paragraph{Computational Resources.}
Model training and evaluation were carried out on RunPod\footnote{\url{https://www.runpod.io}} equipped with an NVIDIA H200 GPU. The architectures were implemented in Python using TensorFlow/Keras, leveraging GPU acceleration for efficient training and inference. The atomistic Monte Carlo state simulations were executed separately under the multi-GPU JAX configuration described in Section~\ref{sec:montecarlo_sim}.

\paragraph{Experiment Tracking.}
The training pipeline was integrated with MLflow\footnote{\url{https://mlflow.org}} for experiment tracking, versioning, and model registry, enabling systematic comparison of architecture variants and hyperparameter configurations as well as full reproducibility across runs.

\paragraph{Data Partitioning.}
The dataset $\mathcal{D}$ was partitioned into training, validation, and test subsets using a two-stage random split with a fixed random seed for reproducibility. First, $15\%$ of the total dataset was isolated as a hold-out test set; from the remaining $85\%$, a further $15\%$ of the total was reserved for validation, yielding an effective $70\%/15\%/15\%$ train/validation/test split. The training set drives gradient-based optimization, the validation set governs early stopping and model selection, and the test set is reserved for final generalization assessment. The fixed seed keeps the samples strictly independent across subsets while preserving the underlying thermodynamic distributions of the data.

\subsection{Development of the Complete Cycle}\label{sec:cycle_development}

This section reports the experimental development of the complete forward--inverse cycle. The first part, presented here, concerns the \emph{inverse problem}: the estimation of the Hamiltonian parameters $\boldsymbol{\theta}$ from magnetic domain images, together with the interpretability metrics that make the learned mappings physically traceable. These results were obtained and consolidated in the published study associated with this thesis. The forward (direct) task that closes the cycle is presented next.

\subsubsection{Thermodynamic Simulation of Magnetic Topologies}
\label{sec:thermo_results}

%parameter kde
The atomistic Hamiltonian formulation and the GPU-accelerated Metropolis--Hastings Monte Carlo with simulated annealing that generate the training data were already described in detail in the dataset section (Sections~\ref{sec:hamiltonian} and~\ref{sec:montecarlo_sim}); we therefore do not repeat that formulation here and summarize only the statistical characterization of the resulting dataset. Figure~\ref{fig:parameter_distributions} delineates the empirical probability density functions---histograms together with kernel density estimates---for the extended parameter space $\boldsymbol{\theta}$. The higher-order exchange constants ($\tilde{J}_{2}$, $\tilde{J}_{3}$, $\tilde{J}_{4}$) concentrate sharply around zero with narrow, nearly symmetric distributions, whereas the DMI strength $\tilde{K}_{DM}$ spans its full $[0,1.2]$~meV range; the initial annealing temperature $T^{(0)}$ is strongly right-skewed, and the anisotropy $\tilde{K}_{an1}$ and external field $\tilde{H}_{ex}$ are peaked near zero with heavy tails toward high, Ising-like regimes. These distributional shapes and sampling heterogeneities govern the topological diversity of the generated magnetic phase space and therefore directly condition the learning dynamics, optimization stability, and predictive limits of the downstream deep-regression frameworks when navigating the highly degenerate regimes of the extended Heisenberg Hamiltonian.

%parameter kde
\begin{figure}[H]
    \centering
    \includegraphics[width=\linewidth]{Figures/ThermodynamicSimulation/parameter_distributions_kde.pdf}
    \caption{Empirical distributions of the thermodynamic and Hamiltonian parameters $\{T^{(0)}, \tilde{J}_2, \tilde{J}_3, \tilde{J}_4, \tilde{K}_{an1}, \tilde{K}_{anS}, \tilde{H}_{ex}, \tilde{K}_{DM}\}$
    used to generate the dataset. Each subplot presents a histogram and KDE for a given variable, along with
    summary statistics including mean, standard deviation, and value range.}
    \label{fig:parameter_distributions}
\end{figure}

\jc{\paragraph{Universality class: from Heisenberg to Ising-like behavior.} An examiner asked whether a model trained only on continuous-symmetry (Heisenberg) physics would collapse or fail when confronted with a discrete, Ising-like ($Z_2$) system whose domains are separated by abrupt rather than smooth walls. This regime is in fact \emph{already spanned} by the training corpus: the anisotropy range is deliberately extended to strongly uniaxial values, which continuously drives the system from the DMI-dominated Heisenberg regime (smooth chiral/helical walls) toward a collinear, Ising-like regime with sharp up/down domain boundaries---the crossover the examiner suggests probing by ``switching off or modifying components.'' The effect is not merely present in the data but is recovered by the estimators: the unsupervised phase analysis of Section~\ref{sec:dl_results} isolates a distinct \emph{Ising phase} (sharp, discrete domain walls at high anisotropy), and it is among the \emph{best}-recovered regimes ($R^2\approx0.94$--$0.99$, alongside the skyrmion phase), rather than a failure mode. The framework therefore transitions smoothly between universality classes by varying the anisotropy, and does not degrade on discrete-symmetry textures; a fully systematic Heisenberg$\to$Ising ablation (forcing extreme uniaxial limits) is nonetheless retained as a validation experiment for the estimation objective.}

\paragraph{Scope note on chirality.}
The Monte Carlo sampling is deliberately restricted to $\tilde{K}_{DM}\geq 0$, consistent with the scope established in the problem statement (Section~\ref{sec:problem_statement}). This restriction sidesteps a known \emph{chirality degeneracy}: a sign reversal $\tilde{K}_{DM}\!\rightarrow\!-\tilde{K}_{DM}$ yields mirror-image spin textures whose out-of-plane projection $s_z$ is nearly indistinguishable, so that the handedness of the DMI is only weakly identifiable from the $s_z$ observable alone. We do not attempt to resolve this handedness ambiguity in the present work; it is acknowledged as an explicit limitation of the single-axis observation model rather than a solved problem, and disentangling it would require additional observables (in-plane components or complementary imaging modalities) beyond the $s_z$ projection used throughout.

The remaining aspects of the thermodynamic simulation were already presented earlier and are not reproduced here to avoid redundancy: the Monte Carlo convergence dynamics and representative ground-state spin configurations of a nanodot during simulated annealing were illustrated in the dataset and temporal-analysis sections (Sections~\ref{sec:montecarlo_sim} and~\ref{sec:temporal_objective}), and the two-dimensional phase diagrams that expose the structural degeneracy of the sampled parameter space---the ill-conditioned foundation for the subsequent inverse estimation---were discussed in the problem statement (Figure~\ref{fig:degeneracy_problem}).

\subsubsection{Inverse Problem: Parameter Estimation Results}\label{sec:dl_results}

The predictive performance of the five evaluated architectures (the
conventional CNN baseline, DenseNet, ResNet, Xception, and ViT) was
assessed on the held-out test set comprising 15\% of the total dataset.
All five models were trained from scratch under an identical protocol
--- without any pretraining or transfer learning --- and with the same
input pipeline. In particular, the single-channel $s_z$ maps were
augmented on the fly through the discrete symmetry operations under
which the Hamiltonian targets $\boldsymbol{\theta}$ are strictly
invariant: random $90^\circ$ \emph{rotations} and \emph{translations}
of the nanodot image. Because every component of $\boldsymbol{\theta}$
is a scalar magnitude, these proper rigid transformations enlarge the
effective sample diversity and enforce the correct rotational and
translational invariance of the estimator while preserving both the
physical parameters and the chiral handedness dictated by the DMI ---
a preprocessing choice that is central to the robustness of the
inverse mapping. Each model inferred the complete extended parameter
vector $\boldsymbol{\theta}$ directly from the magnetization image
$\mathbf{X}_n$, without any auxiliary physical information.

As shown in Figures~\ref{fig:r2_metrics} and~\ref{fig:mae_metrics}, all
five architectures perform competitively, with the differences on the
identifiable parameters typically confined to a few percent of $R^2$.
Among them, Xception emerges as the strongest overall model: it attains
the highest $R^2$ for six of the eight parameters --- including the
most identifiable ones, $T^{(0)}$, $\tilde{J}_2$, $\tilde{K}_{an1}$,
and $\tilde{K}_{DM}$ --- while the ViT marginally leads on the external
field $\tilde{H}_{ex}$ and the conventional CNN baseline on the surface
anisotropy $\tilde{K}_{anS}$. The same ranking is largely mirrored in
the MAE panels, where Xception yields the lowest absolute error for the
physically dominant parameters $T^{(0)}$, $\tilde{K}_{an1}$, and
$\tilde{K}_{DM}$. A particularly informative observation is that the
conventional CNN baseline --- trained from scratch and devoid of
pretraining or specialized inductive biases --- matches, and for some
parameters even exceeds, the deeper backbones. This confirms that
neither transfer learning from natural images nor the sophisticated
residual, dense, depthwise, or attention mechanisms provide a decisive
advantage for this task; the estimation accuracy is instead governed by
the physical information that the $s_z$ projection makes available.
Physically, Xception's slight edge stems from its depthwise separable
convolution mechanism, which efficiently decouples the extraction of
spatial spin-texture frequencies from the cross-channel correlations
required to map those features onto the Hamiltonian parameter space,
yielding the most accurate point predictions for the dominantly
identifiable parameters. The ViT, in turn, remains competitive by
processing $\mathbf{X}_n$ as a sequence of non-overlapping spatial
patches and computing multi-head self-attention across all patch pairs,
explicitly capturing the long-range correlations --- such as the global
chirality imposed by $\tilde{K}_{DM}$ and the large-scale ordering
driven by $\tilde{J}_1$ --- that are inaccessible to purely local
convolutional receptive fields.

{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\paragraph{Model size versus predictive accuracy.}
This near-equivalence across architectures raises the question of
whether model capacity limits the estimation at all.
Figure~\ref{fig:accuracy_vs_size} plots the mean $R^2$ over the
identifiable parameters against the number of trainable weights of each
architecture, spanning nearly two orders of magnitude in model size ---
from the compact conventional CNN ($\sim\!1$~M parameters) to the ViT
($\sim\!86$~M parameters). The resulting trend is essentially flat: the
conventional CNN already attains a mean $R^2$ close to that of the
largest models, and increasing the parameter count by two orders of
magnitude yields only a marginal improvement, with Xception offering the
best accuracy--size trade-off. This saturation demonstrates that
predictive accuracy is not bottlenecked by model capacity but by the
physical information accessible in the $s_z$ projection: once an
architecture is expressive enough to read the relevant spatial features,
additional parameters and more elaborate inductive biases provide
diminishing returns. This finding has a direct methodological
consequence for the present work: it justifies starting the
forward--inverse framework from \emph{lightweight, less complex
architectures} rather than defaulting to large pretrained backbones,
since the recovery of the identifiable parameters is a robust,
architecture-independent property of the data --- a point that motivates
the comparative study of Objective~1 (Section~\ref{sec:cycle_consistency}).}

\begin{figure}[H]
    \centering
    \includegraphics[width=0.8\linewidth]{Figures/Regression/accuracy_vs_size.pdf}
    \caption{Mean $R^2$ over the identifiable Hamiltonian parameters
    as a function of the number of trainable weights (log scale) for
    the five evaluated architectures. The nearly flat trend --- with the
    compact conventional CNN already matching the accuracy of models
    two orders of magnitude larger --- indicates that predictive accuracy
    is limited by the physical information available in the $s_z$
    projection rather than by model capacity.}
    \label{fig:accuracy_vs_size}
\end{figure}

Figures~\ref{fig:r2_metrics} and~\ref{fig:mae_metrics} reveal a
pronounced and physically interpretable heterogeneity in predictive
accuracy across the parameter space. The initial annealing temperature
$T^{(0)}$, the second-shell exchange constant $\tilde{J}_2$, and the
DMI strength $\tilde{K}_{DM}$ consistently yield the highest $R^2$
scores across all architectures, reflecting the robust and spatially
distinctive topological imprints they leave on the observable
magnetization texture: $T^{(0)}$ governs the global degree of spin
order, $\tilde{J}_2$ controls the second-neighbor exchange pathway
that modulates the domain-scale collinearity, and $\tilde{K}_{DM}$
directly dictates the characteristic wavelength and chirality of the
domain patterns. However, a seemingly contradictory observation
emerges when examining the MAE panels in Figure~\ref{fig:mae_metrics}:
despite their high $R^2$ scores, $T^{(0)}$, $\tilde{J}_2$, and
$\tilde{K}_{DM}$ also exhibit comparatively larger absolute errors in
their respective physical units --- reaching up to approximately
$0.49\,\mathrm{K}$ for $T^{(0)}$, $0.021\,\mathrm{meV}$ for
$\tilde{J}_2$, and $0.037\,\mathrm{meV}$ for $\tilde{K}_{DM}$ in the
worst-performing architecture. This apparent inconsistency is not a
failure of the models but rather a direct consequence of the
thermodynamic landscape of the dataset. As illustrated in the phase diagrams of Figure~\ref{fig:degeneracy_problem}, the parameter space
contains extensive high-temperature regions where thermal
fluctuations above the effective Curie temperature $T_C$ collapse the
magnetization texture into an indistinguishable paramagnetic noise
phase, regardless of the specific values of $\tilde{J}_2$ or
$\tilde{K}_{DM}$. Within these thermally disordered regimes, visually
identical configurations correspond to widely different parameter
values, rendering the inverse mapping fundamentally ill-posed and
inflating the absolute prediction error even when the global
correlation structure --- captured by $R^2$ --- remains strong. The
MAE values for $T^{(0)}$, $\tilde{J}_2$, and $\tilde{K}_{DM}$
therefore reflect the intrinsic degeneracy of the high-temperature
paramagnetic phase rather than a systematic modeling deficiency: the
same parameter that is most accurately recovered in the ordered
low-temperature regime (where the spin texture preserves a clear
topological signature) is simultaneously the most degenerate in the
disordered high-temperature regime (where the texture collapses into
noise), producing non-negligible global MAE values driven primarily
by the ill-conditioned subset of the dataset. A rigorous quantitative
decomposition of this effect requires first separating the dataset
into its constituent magnetic structural categories, as the global
aggregation of all configurations inevitably conflates high-accuracy
ordered-phase predictions with fundamentally ill-posed
paramagnetic-regime estimates. This per-phase analysis is addressed
further ahead, where the unsupervised structural clustering of the
magnetization images allows us to isolate and quantify the specific
contribution of each magnetic phase to the observed prediction error.

{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\paragraph{Outlook: resolving the collapsed regimes.}
It is important to stress that the elevated errors in the
high-temperature, Curie-dominated and otherwise degenerate regions are
\emph{not} treated here as a definitive ceiling, but as the baseline
against which the mechanisms proposed in this thesis are to be measured.
Two complementary routes are expected to alleviate them and will be
re-evaluated once implemented. First, the results reported above come
from the inverse regressor trained in isolation; under the
cycle-consistent forward--inverse coupling of Objective~1
(Section~\ref{sec:cycle_consistency}), the closed-loop gradient
re-adjusts the estimator so that its predictions must also regenerate a
consistent image, discouraging the collapse toward a degenerate mean in
precisely these ill-posed regions. Second, and more fundamentally, these
regimes are ambiguous only under a \emph{single static} $s_z$ snapshot;
the temporal state sequence of Objective~2
(Section~\ref{sec:temporal_objective}) supplies the transition dynamics
that disambiguate configurations sharing a similar instantaneous
texture. Accordingly, the transition-aware latent analysis --- and in
particular PHATE, whose trajectory-preserving embedding is designed for
exactly this continuum --- will be evaluated only once the time series is
incorporated, since its advantage over the cluster-oriented UMAP used
here becomes meaningful only when the temporal transitions between states
are available.}

Conversely, the models exhibit two qualitatively distinct regimes of
limited predictive performance for the remaining Hamiltonian
parameters, which together delimit the fundamental identifiability
boundaries of the inverse problem in the 2D observation space. The
higher-order exchange constants $\tilde{J}_3$ and $\tilde{J}_4$
constitute the most severe case: their $R^2$ scores remain near zero
across all evaluated architectures, indicating that the models are
unable to recover meaningful information about these parameters
beyond the dataset mean. {\color[rgb]{0,0,1}Physically, this is a direct consequence of
the \emph{interaction distance}: $\tilde{J}_3$ and $\tilde{J}_4$ are
third- and fourth-neighbour exchange couplings, so their effect is
distributed over spin pairs separated by increasingly large lattice
distances. As the interaction range grows, the corresponding energetic
contribution is both weaker and more delocalized, and it is
additionally dominated by the primary $\tilde{J}_1$ and $\tilde{J}_2$
interactions; the net result is that these long-range couplings leave
no distinctive, spatially localized imprint on the equilibrium texture.
Once that texture is projected onto the two-dimensional $s_z$ image,
whatever residual signature they might carry falls below the
observable scale, so there is simply \emph{no remnant capturable
information} in the state images from which the network could recover
them --- rendering $\tilde{J}_3$ and $\tilde{J}_4$ effectively
unidentifiable from the $s_z$ projection alone.} Notably, however, their MAE values --- on the order of
$0.03$--$0.05\,\mathrm{meV}$ --- remain numerically comparable to
those of the better-identified parameters, which is a direct
consequence of their narrow physical range in the dataset rather than
genuine predictive skill: a model predicting the dataset mean for
$\tilde{J}_3$ and $\tilde{J}_4$ will naturally incur a small absolute
error simply because the dynamic range of these parameters is itself
limited. The magnetocrystalline anisotropy constants $\tilde{K}_{an1}$
and $\tilde{K}_{anS}$ together with the external Zeeman field
$\tilde{H}_{ex}$, in turn, occupy an intermediate identifiability
regime. {\color[rgb]{0,0,1}This partial predictability is rooted in a limitation of the
\emph{observation axis} itself: the estimator sees only the
out-of-plane component $s_z$, yet the anisotropy and field terms couple
most strongly to the in-plane spin components $s_x$ and $s_y$, which
are precisely the components discarded by the single-axis projection.
In other words, the variable that would most directly encode these
parameters is not the one being observed, so the models can recover
them only indirectly, through the secondary topological imprints they
induce on the chiral domain structure --- the modulation of stripe
periodicity or the preferential orientation of skyrmion cores. This is
the same observational incompleteness that underlies the chirality
degeneracy discussed earlier (the sign of $\tilde{K}_{DM}$ being
invisible to $s_z$): whenever the physically dominant component lies
in the plane, the $s_z$-only view collapses distinct configurations
into similar observables. Furthermore, the visual manifestations of
surface anisotropy $\tilde{K}_{anS}$ remain partially entangled with
bulk anisotropy $\tilde{K}_{an1}$ and geometrically confined to the
boundary layer of the nanodot, while the Zeeman contribution
$\tilde{H}_{ex}$ --- with MAE values below $0.009\,\mathrm{meV/atom}$
across all architectures --- becomes increasingly indistinguishable
from thermal disorder in the high-temperature regime. Together, these
effects render the anisotropy and field parameters intrinsically
partially observable and define the fundamental identifiability limits
of the inverse problem within the two-dimensional $s_z$ projection. We
note, however, that these limits are stated for the \emph{static,
single-snapshot} estimator evaluated here; whether part of this
in-plane information can be recovered indirectly is one of the
questions to be revisited once the forward--inverse cycle enforces
physical consistency (Objective~1, Section~\ref{sec:cycle_consistency})
and, especially, once the temporal series of states adds the transition
dynamics that a single $s_z$ frame cannot provide (Objective~2,
Section~\ref{sec:temporal_objective}).}

\begin{figure}[H]
    \centering
    \includegraphics[width=\linewidth]
    {Figures/Regression/model_comparison_r2.pdf}
    \caption{Per-variable $R^2$ score across the five evaluated
    architectures (conventional CNN, DenseNet, ResNet, Xception, and
    ViT) for the eight Hamiltonian parameters
    $\boldsymbol{\theta}$, reported on the held-out test set. Each
    panel corresponds to one target parameter and shows one bar per
    architecture. Negative $R^2$ values,
    indicating predictions no better than the dataset mean, are
    clipped to zero for visualization clarity. Black-bordered bars
    indicate the best-performing architecture for each parameter.}
    \label{fig:r2_metrics}
\end{figure}

\begin{figure}[H]
    \centering
    \includegraphics[width=\linewidth]
    {Figures/Regression/model_comparison_mae.pdf}
    \caption{Per-variable Mean Absolute Error (MAE) across the five
    evaluated architectures for the eight Hamiltonian parameters
    $\boldsymbol{\theta}$, reported on the held-out test set in the
    original physical units of each parameter (K for $T^{(0)}$, meV
    for exchange and DMI constants, and meV/atom for anisotropy and
    external field parameters). Each panel corresponds to one target
    parameter and shows one bar per architecture. The comparatively
    larger MAE observed for $T^{(0)}$, $\tilde{J}_2$, and
    $\tilde{K}_{DM}$ --- reaching up to $0.49\,\mathrm{K}$,
    $0.021\,\mathrm{meV}$, and $0.037\,\mathrm{meV}$ respectively in
    the worst-performing architecture --- is attributable to the
    thermally disordered paramagnetic configurations populating the
    high-temperature regime of the dataset (see
    Figure~\ref{fig:degeneracy_problem}), where configurations
    generated by widely different parameter values relax into
    statistically similar noise textures, inflating the absolute error
    even when the global $R^2$ correlation remains strong. The
    comparatively small MAE of $\tilde{J}_3$ and $\tilde{J}_4$ despite
    their near-zero $R^2$ reflects the narrow dynamic range of these
    parameters in the dataset rather than genuine predictive skill.
    Black-bordered bars indicate the best-performing architecture
    for each parameter.}
    \label{fig:mae_metrics}
\end{figure}

%umap visualization
To gain deeper insight into the internal representations learned by
the best-performing model, Xception, during the inverse Hamiltonian
estimation task, Figure~\ref{fig:umap_latent} presents a Uniform
Manifold Approximation and Projection (UMAP) of the model's latent
space~\cite{ghojogh2021uniform}, where each point corresponds to a
test sample colored by the value of each of the eight Hamiltonian
parameters $\boldsymbol{\theta}$. The UMAP embedding was computed
from the high-dimensional feature vectors extracted from the Xception
backbone prior to the regression head, providing a two-dimensional
visualization of the geometric structure that the model has learned
to impose on the magnetization image manifold.
Rather than forming a single undifferentiated cloud, the latent
space exhibits a rich topological structure composed of several
geometrically distinct clusters and continuous gradients, reflecting
the fact that the model has organized the magnetization textures
according to their underlying physical regimes. Examining the
coloring by $T^{(0)}$ reveals the clearest and most spatially
coherent gradient across the embedding: low-temperature ordered
configurations concentrate in well-separated compact regions, while
high-temperature disordered states occupy a large, diffuse cluster
in the upper portion of the latent space, consistent with the strong
predictive performance of the model on $T^{(0)}$. The DMI strength
$\tilde{K}_{DM}$ also produces a structured gradient in the latent
space, with high-$\tilde{K}_{DM}$ helical configurations forming a
distinct elongated region separated from the low-$\tilde{K}_{DM}$
disordered states, confirming that Xception has successfully encoded
the chiral winding imposed by the DMI as a geometrically meaningful
direction in its latent representation. The bulk anisotropy
$\tilde{K}_{an1}$ and the external field $\tilde{H}_{ex}$ likewise
display coherent, structured gradients across the embedding, mirroring
their high regression accuracy and confirming that Xception encodes
them as well-defined directions in its latent geometry. In contrast,
the longer-range exchange constants $\tilde{J}_3$ and $\tilde{J}_4$
produce nearly uniform colorings across the entire embedding,
indicating that the model's internal representations carry little
discriminative information about these parameters --- a result that
is fully consistent with their low $R^2$ values and their energetic
subordination to the dominant first-shell exchange
$\tilde{J}_1 = 1.0$~meV. The surface anisotropy $\tilde{K}_{anS}$
occupies an intermediate case, showing a partially mixed coloring
consistent with its moderate identifiability and its entanglement
with the bulk anisotropy signature.

%umap 2D
Beyond the per-parameter gradients, the same embedding reveals that
Xception has organized the magnetization textures into geometrically
distinct and physically interpretable regions, without any explicit
supervision of magnetic phase labels (Figure~\ref{fig:umap_images_only}). The
systematic variation of $T^{(0)}$ separates ordered, low-temperature
configurations --- which concentrate in compact, well-separated regions
--- from the thermally disordered states that occupy a large, diffuse
cluster at high $T^{(0)}$, where thermal fluctuations dominate over the
competing energetic contributions of $\tilde{J}_1$ and $\tilde{K}_{DM}$.
Superimposed on this temperature axis, the $\tilde{K}_{DM}$ gradient
further resolves the ordered region into the chiral helical, dense
skyrmionic, and collinear Ising-like regimes, while the mixed-chirality
configurations form their own transitional zone. This spontaneous
geometric separation motivates the structured clustering analysis
performed next.

%UMAP ViT
\begin{figure}[H]
    \centering
    \includegraphics[width=1\linewidth]
    {Figures/Regression/latent_xception_umap.pdf}
    \caption{UMAP-based 2D projection of the Xception latent space
    on the held-out test set, colored by each of the eight
    Hamiltonian parameters $\boldsymbol{\theta} = [T^{(0)},
    \tilde{J}_2, \tilde{J}_3, \tilde{J}_4, \tilde{K}_{an1},
    \tilde{K}_{anS}, \tilde{H}_{ex}, \tilde{K}_{DM}]$. Each
    point represents a magnetization image $\mathbf{X}_n$
    projected from the high-dimensional feature space of the
    Xception backbone into two dimensions via UMAP.}
    \label{fig:umap_latent}
\end{figure}

%umap 2D
\begin{figure}[H]
    \centering
    \includegraphics[width=1\linewidth]
    {Figures/latent_umap_states-2.pdf}
    \caption{Spatial distribution of magnetization images $\mathbf{X}_n$ across the two-dimensional UMAP projection of the Xception latent space (test set). Each thumbnail represents the out-of-plane magnetization field $s_z$ of a randomly selected test sample placed at its corresponding UMAP coordinate, with the underlying grey points showing the full embedding. The spontaneous organization of the textures into geometrically separated regions---ordered helical and skyrmionic domains, collinear Ising-like states, and the diffuse cluster of thermally disordered configurations---confirms that Xception encodes the magnetic phases without any explicit supervision.}
    \label{fig:umap_images_only}
\end{figure}

To systematically identify and characterize the dominant magnetic 
phases encoded in the latent space, a density-based clustering 
analysis was performed using the HDBSCAN algorithm on the UMAP 
embedding, followed by a KMeans subdivision of anomalously large 
clusters. Based on visual inspection of representative samples 
and the mean values of the Hamiltonian parameters 
$\boldsymbol{\theta}$ within each group, the resulting clusters
were consolidated into five physically meaningful magnetic phase
categories.

The \textit{helical phase} contains the most spatially coherent and regularly ordered configurations in the dataset, displaying well-defined stripe and labyrinthine patterns with consistent chiral winding direction and spatial periodicity across the nanodot, stabilized at low annealing temperature by a moderate DMI ($\langle\tilde{K}_{DM}\rangle \approx 0.75$~meV). The \textit{skyrmion phase} groups configurations in which the strong DMI ($\langle\tilde{K}_{DM}\rangle \approx 0.88$~meV) stabilizes dense lattices of isolated, radially symmetric topological solitons, clearly visible as arrays of circular cores of reversed $s_z$ embedded in a uniform background. The \textit{Ising phase} comprises the low-temperature ($\langle T^{(0)}\rangle \approx 1.86$~K), high-anisotropy configurations characterized by sharp, discrete domain walls separating collinear up/down regions, reflecting the deliberately extended anisotropy range that drives the system toward an Ising-like regime. The \textit{bimeron phase} gathers mixed-chirality textures in which in-plane and out-of-plane components coexist as paired meron--antimeron structures, forming irregular large-scale domains without the long-range periodicity of the helical phase. Finally, the \textit{others} category constitutes the most heterogeneous group, gathering thermally disordered states in which thermal fluctuations have disrupted long-range spin order, transitional configurations located at the boundaries between competing phases, and topologically mixed textures whose spatial structure is ambiguous. The common characteristic of this group is the absence of a distinctive and reproducible magnetization pattern, making them the most structurally ill-conditioned samples in the dataset.

Figure~\ref{fig:structure_representatives} presents five 
representative magnetization images per phase, selected as the 
samples closest to the centroid of each group in the UMAP 
embedding, along with the mean Hamiltonian parameters 
$\langle T^{(0)}\rangle$, $\langle\tilde{J}_2\rangle$, and 
$\langle\tilde{K}_{DM}\rangle$ characterizing each phase.

\begin{figure}[H]
    \centering
    \includegraphics[width=0.85\linewidth, height=0.66\textheight,
    keepaspectratio]
    {Figures/Regression/structure_representatives.pdf}
    \caption{Representative magnetization images $\mathbf{X}_n$
    for each of the five magnetic phase categories identified
    through clustering of the Xception latent space.
    Each row displays five samples selected as the nearest
    neighbors to the cluster centroid in the UMAP embedding.
    Mean Hamiltonian parameters $\langle T^{(0)}\rangle$,
    $\langle\tilde{J}_2\rangle$, and $\langle\tilde{K}_{DM}\rangle$
    are reported below each row. The Helical phase presents ordered,
    spatially coherent stripe and labyrinthine configurations with
    consistent periodicity and chiral winding. The Others category
    gathers thermally disordered, transitional, and topologically
    mixed configurations that lack a defining spatial signature. The
    Bimeron phase shows mixed-chirality textures with coexisting
    in-plane and out-of-plane components forming irregular domains.
    The Skyrmion phase displays dense lattices of isolated, radially
    symmetric topological solitons stabilized by a strong DMI. The
    Ising phase exhibits sharp, discrete domain walls between
    collinear up/down regions, characteristic of the high-anisotropy
    regime.}
    \label{fig:structure_representatives}
\end{figure}

The per-phase regression performance of the best-performing model,
Xception, evaluated on the test samples used for the clustering
analysis, is summarized in Figures~\ref{fig:metrics_by_structure}
and~\ref{fig:mae_by_structure}. The results reveal a pronounced
heterogeneity in predictive accuracy across magnetic phases that
is physically consistent with the identifiability analysis
presented earlier in this section. The \textit{skyrmion} and
\textit{Ising} phases achieve the highest $R^2$ values (typically
$0.94$--$0.99$) and the lowest MAE across essentially all
identifiable parameters, reflecting the fact that these highly
ordered configurations --- dense soliton lattices and sharp,
discrete domain walls, respectively --- exhibit the most
discriminative and spatially coherent signatures in the $s_z$
projection. Within these phases, the well-defined periodicity and
topological structure of the magnetization texture provide
unambiguous observational constraints that allow the model to
recover the underlying Hamiltonian parameters with high fidelity.
The \textit{helical} phase follows closely, with excellent recovery
of $T^{(0)}$ and $\tilde{K}_{DM}$ ($R^2 \approx 0.99$) and good
performance for $\tilde{J}_2$ and $\tilde{H}_{ex}$, although its
anisotropy constants are estimated less accurately owing to the
weaker anisotropic imprint in the chiral stripe regime.

In stark contrast, the \textit{others} and \textit{bimeron}
categories concentrate the largest absolute errors across
virtually all Hamiltonian parameters, as evidenced by the dominant
MAE values in Figure~\ref{fig:mae_by_structure}. This result
directly confirms the physical interpretation advanced earlier:
the thermally disordered configurations grouped in the
\textit{others} category, and the mixed-chirality meron--antimeron
textures of the \textit{bimeron} phase, relax into structurally
weakly-discriminative magnetization patterns, so that widely
different parameter combinations produce statistically similar
textures and inflate the inverse-mapping error within these
regimes. The elevated MAE for $T^{(0)}$ in the \textit{others}
phase is particularly revealing, as it quantitatively isolates the
contribution of the high-temperature paramagnetic regime to the
global prediction error observed in the aggregated analysis;
likewise, the \textit{bimeron} phase yields the lowest $R^2$ for
the anisotropy and field parameters, reflecting the intrinsic
spatial ambiguity of its coexisting in-plane and out-of-plane
components.

Across all five phases, the higher-order exchange constants
$\tilde{J}_3$ and $\tilde{J}_4$ retain near-zero $R^2$, confirming
at the per-phase level that their unidentifiability is a
fundamental property of the $s_z$ observation rather than an
artifact of any particular structural regime.

\begin{figure}[H]
    \centering
    \includegraphics[width=\linewidth, keepaspectratio]
    {Figures/Regression/metrics_by_structure_r2.pdf}
    \caption{Per-phase $R^2$ score of the best-performing model,
    Xception, across all eight Hamiltonian parameters
    $\boldsymbol{\theta}$ for the five identified magnetic
    phase categories. Each bar group
    corresponds to one magnetic phase and each bar to one
    target parameter. Dashed horizontal lines mark the
    identifiability thresholds at $R^2 = 0.85$ (high
    predictability) and $R^2 = 0.50$ (partial predictability).
    Negative $R^2$ values and near-zero variance cases (N/A)
    are clipped to zero. The Skyrmion and Ising phases achieve
    near-perfect accuracy across the identifiable parameters,
    while the Bimeron and Others phases exhibit the lowest
    identifiability owing to their weakly discriminative,
    structurally ambiguous magnetization textures.}
    \label{fig:metrics_by_structure}
\end{figure}

\begin{figure}[H]
    \centering
    \includegraphics[width=\linewidth, keepaspectratio]
    {Figures/Regression/metrics_by_structure_mae_grid.pdf}
    \caption{Per-phase Mean Absolute Error (MAE) of the
    best-performing model, Xception, across all eight Hamiltonian
    parameters $\boldsymbol{\theta}$ for the five identified magnetic
    phase categories, reported in the
    original physical units of each parameter after inverse
    Min-Max scaling. Each panel corresponds to one target
    parameter and shows one bar per magnetic phase. The
    \textit{others} and \textit{bimeron} categories consistently
    concentrate the largest absolute errors across all parameters,
    confirming that the thermally disordered, transitional, and
    mixed-chirality configurations within these groups constitute the
    primary source of prediction error in the global regression
    analysis. Black-bordered bars indicate the magnetic phase
    with the lowest MAE for each parameter.}
    \label{fig:mae_by_structure}
\end{figure}

\subsubsection{Interpretability Results via RAMs}
\label{sec:rams_results}

%\subsubsection{Spatial Interpretability via Xception}
To spatially trace the predictive behavior of the Xception
architecture across the identified magnetic phase categories,
RAMs were computed following the
CNN-based formulation introduced in
Section~\ref{sec:CNN_RAMs}. For each of the five magnetic
phases a single representative magnetization image
$\mathbf{X}_n$ was selected as the sample closest to the
centroid of the corresponding cluster group in the UMAP
latent space, maximizing the concentration of gradient
activations within the physical extent of the nanodot disk.
RAMs were computed for the three most identifiable
Hamiltonian parameters ($T^{(0)}$, $\tilde{J}_2$, and
$\tilde{K}_{DM}$) across eight strategically selected
convolutional layers of the Xception backbone, spanning the
full depth of the network:

\begin{itemize}
    \item[--] \texttt{block1\_conv2}: entry-level features
    at spatial resolution $112\times112$, capturing
    low-level pixel-scale magnetization gradients.
    \item[--] \texttt{block3\_sepconv2} and
    \texttt{block4\_sepconv2}: intermediate entry-flow
    layers at $56\times56$ and $28\times28$, encoding
    domain boundary frequencies and local spin-winding
    patterns.
    \item[--] \texttt{block6\_sepconv2} through
    \texttt{block12\_sepconv2}: middle-flow layers at
    $14\times14$, constituting the deepest repeated
    processing stage of Xception and encoding progressively
    more abstract topological representations of the
    magnetic texture.
    \item[--] \texttt{block13\_sepconv2}: exit-flow
    transition layer prior to global pooling, encoding
    high-level semantic representations of the global
    magnetic phase.
\end{itemize}

\paragraph{Selection of the precision factor $\gamma$.}
The precision factor $\gamma$ in the RAM objective
$\mathcal{O}_p = \exp\!\left(-\gamma\left(\theta_p -
\hat{\theta}_p\right)^2\right)$ governs the selectivity
of the gradient signal: small values render the objective
nearly constant regardless of prediction quality, producing
spatially uninformative maps, while excessively large values
collapse the gradient to zero for parameters with
non-negligible prediction error, yielding empty activation
maps. To select $\gamma$ in a principled and data-driven
manner, a sensitivity analysis was conducted by evaluating
the objective $\mathcal{O}_p$ at the empirical mean
prediction error $\bar{\varepsilon}_p =
\langle|\theta_p - \hat{\theta}_p|\rangle$ of each
Hamiltonian parameter on the test set, for values spanning
$\gamma \in \{0.1, 0.3, 0.5, 1, 2, 5, 10, 20, 50, 100\}$.

Figure~\ref{fig:gamma_selection} illustrates the resulting
sensitivity curves. The left panel shows the shape of the
Gaussian objective as a function of the prediction error
$\varepsilon$ for each candidate $\gamma$: values below
$\gamma = 1$ produce flat curves that activate uniformly
regardless of prediction quality, while values above
$\gamma = 20$ generate overly steep curves that suppress
the gradient for errors as small as $\varepsilon \approx
0.2$. The right panel evaluates $\mathcal{O}_p$ at the
empirical mean error of each parameter: all variables
maintain $\mathcal{O}_p > 0.5$ for $\gamma \leq 10$,
whereas for $\gamma \geq 20$ the parameters with larger
prediction errors --- specifically $T^{(0)}$ and
$\tilde{K}_{DM}$, whose mean errors reach $0.17$ and
$0.24$ respectively --- begin to approach the gradient
collapse threshold of $\mathcal{O}_p < 0.05$. The value
$\gamma = 10$ was therefore selected as the optimal
operating point: it is the most selective value that
simultaneously maintains a well-conditioned gradient
signal for all eight Hamiltonian parameters, ensuring
that the resulting RAMs are both spatially discriminative
and physically interpretable across the full
identifiability spectrum of the inverse problem.

\begin{figure}[H]
    \centering
    \includegraphics[width=\linewidth, keepaspectratio]
    {Figures/Regression/gamma_gaussian_shape.pdf}
    \caption{Sensitivity analysis of the RAM precision
    factor $\gamma$. Left: shape of the Gaussian objective
    $\mathcal{O}_p = e^{-\gamma\varepsilon^2}$ as a
    function of the prediction error
    $\varepsilon = |\theta_p - \hat{\theta}_p|$ for
    $\gamma \in \{0.1, \dots, 100\}$; the selected value
    $\gamma = 10$ is highlighted in red. Vertical dotted
    lines indicate the empirical mean errors
    $\bar{\varepsilon}$ of $T^{(0)}$ and
    $\tilde{K}_{DM}$ on the test set. Right:
    $\mathcal{O}_p$ evaluated at $\bar{\varepsilon}_p$
    for each Hamiltonian parameter as a function of
    $\gamma$. The horizontal dotted line marks the
    gradient collapse threshold $\mathcal{O}_p = 0.05$.
    For $\gamma \leq 10$ all parameters maintain
    $\mathcal{O}_p > 0.5$; for $\gamma \geq 20$ the
    parameters with larger prediction errors begin to
    approach the collapse threshold. The value $\gamma =
    10$ is selected as the most selective operating point
    within the stable gradient regime.}
    \label{fig:gamma_selection}
\end{figure}

To ensure that activation magnitudes remain physically
interpretable and spatially confined to the nanodot
geometry, each RAM was masked by a circular binary mask
aligned with the disk boundary prior to normalization.
The globally normalized RAMs
$\tilde{\mathcal{R}}_{\text{RAM}}^{(l,p)}$ were obtained
following Equation~\ref{eq:RAM_Global_Norm}, and the
spatial patterns were additionally visualized under local
per-cell normalization to reveal the gradient attribution
structure at each individual layer.

Figure~\ref{fig:rams_xception_unified} presents the
resulting RAM overlays for the skyrmion phase, selected for
visualization because its dense lattice of isolated,
radially symmetric solitons produces the clearest and most
physically interpretable gradient attribution patterns,
providing the strongest validation of the hierarchical
decoupling hypothesis. The spatial attribution patterns
reveal a physically consistent progression across network
depth. In the early entry-flow layers
(\texttt{block1\_conv2}, \texttt{block3\_sepconv2}), the
activations for $\tilde{K}_{DM}$ are spatially dense and
sharply structured, coinciding with the individual skyrmion
cores and their boundaries --- precisely the chiral features
that encode the DMI strength. As depth increases through the
middle-flow layers (\texttt{block6} through
\texttt{block12}), these activations progressively
consolidate into coarser, spatially localized hotspots
centered on the topologically significant skyrmion cores,
while the $T^{(0)}$ attribution spreads over the inter-core
background that reflects the global degree of thermal order.
In the terminal exit-flow layer (\texttt{block13\_sepconv2}),
activations become concentrated in a small number of compact
regions, indicating that the network's final semantic
representations encode global topological stability rather
than local texture periodicity. {\color[rgb]{0,0,1}A particularly telling
observation is the $\tilde{J}_2$ row, which is \emph{entirely
inactive} across every layer, for the representative skyrmion
sample whose ground-truth value is exactly
$\tilde{J}_2 = 0.00$~meV. This blank map should not be read as
a failure of the attribution but as a physically correct
\emph{null} result, and it admits two mutually reinforcing
explanations. Physically, at $\tilde{J}_2 = 0$ the second-shell
exchange contributes nothing to the energetic balance that
shapes the skyrmion lattice, so there is no spatial feature
whose modification would alter the $\tilde{J}_2$ estimate ---
there is simply nothing to attribute. Methodologically, because
the network correctly predicts $\tilde{J}_2 \approx 0$ with
small error, the Gaussian objective
$\mathcal{O}_p = \exp(-\gamma(\theta_p-\hat{\theta}_p)^2)$ sits
at its saturated maximum, where its gradient --- and hence the
RAM weights --- vanishes. Crucially, the interpretation is
positive: rather than fabricating a spurious spatial dependence
for a parameter that is physically silent in this phase, the
model looks for $\tilde{J}_2$ evidence \emph{nowhere}, precisely
because there is none to be found. The blank $\tilde{J}_2$ map is
therefore direct evidence that the network has learned the
skyrmion dynamics faithfully --- distinguishing a
physics-grounded estimator from one that would latch onto
arbitrary texture correlations.} This hierarchical progression
matches the theoretical prediction of Section~\ref{sec:Nor_RAMs}:
early layers resolve the short-range energetic competition
between $\tilde{J}_1$ and $\tilde{K}_{DM}$, while deeper layers
integrate these local features into macro-scale thermodynamic
representations governed by $T^{(0)}$.

\begin{figure}[H]
    \centering
    \includegraphics[width=\linewidth,
    height=0.55\textheight, keepaspectratio]
    {Figures/Regression/rams_xception_overlay_Skyrmiones.pdf}
    \caption{RAMs
    $\tilde{\mathcal{R}}_{\text{RAM}}^{(l,p)}$ for the
    Xception architecture, shown for the skyrmion phase,
    selected as the most representative case from the
    five-phase RAM computation. Three Hamiltonian parameters
    $\{T^{(0)}, \tilde{J}_2, \tilde{K}_{DM}\}$ are
    shown (rows). Each panel displays the RAM overlay (jet
    colormap) on the original $s_z$ magnetization image
    (leftmost column), with local per-cell normalization to
    reveal spatial attribution patterns at each layer
    independently. Columns correspond to network layers from
    \texttt{block1} (entry, $112\times112$) to
    \texttt{block13} (exit, $14\times14$). The numerical
    score below each RAM cell reports the fraction of
    activation concentrated within the nanodot disk
    boundary, and the value below each original image
    reports the ground-truth parameter value for that
    representative sample. The $\tilde{K}_{DM}$ activations
    localize on the skyrmion cores, while the $\tilde{J}_2$ row
    is entirely inactive --- the physically correct null
    response for a parameter whose ground-truth value is
    $0.00$~meV in this phase, evidencing that the network does
    not fabricate a spurious dependence where none exists. The
    maps illustrate a physically consistent hierarchical
    decoupling: early layers capture the chiral core
    structure while deeper layers consolidate activations
    into topologically significant spatial hotspots.}
    \label{fig:rams_xception_unified}
\end{figure}

To quantify the relative importance of each network layer
and each Hamiltonian parameter in driving the gradient
response, Figure~\ref{fig:rams_xception_bars} presents
the mean maximum RAM activation per layer and variable,
averaged over $N=300$ randomly sampled test images per
magnetic phase. To enable meaningful cross-layer and
cross-parameter comparisons, the raw activation values
were standardized via a global z-score transform across
all layers and variables within each phase, and subsequently
mapped through a sigmoid function
$\sigma\!\left(\frac{x - \mu}{\sigma_x}\right) \in (0,1)$,
where $\mu$ and $\sigma_x$ denote the global mean and
standard deviation of the raw activations for each variable.
This normalization preserves the relative ordering of
activation magnitudes while compressing outliers and
centering the scale around $\sigma = 0.5$, such that
values above this threshold indicate above-average gradient
response for that parameter at that layer depth.

This statistical analysis confirms and extends the
qualitative observations from the spatial maps. Across
all five phases, the sigmoid-normalized activation profile
exhibits a non-monotonic behavior across depth, with a
consistently dominant peak at the terminal exit-flow layer
\texttt{block13\_sepconv2}, where the network aggregates the
high-level semantic representations of the identifiable
parameters ($T^{(0)}$, $\tilde{J}_2$, $\tilde{K}_{DM}$,
$\tilde{K}_{an1}$, and $\tilde{H}_{ex}$). Secondary peaks in
the middle-flow layers (\texttt{block6}--\texttt{block8})
reflect the intermediate stage at which the localized chiral
and domain-wall features are assembled, and are most
pronounced in the ordered skyrmion and Ising phases whose
sharp textures provide the strongest spatial signal. By
contrast, the higher-order exchange constants $\tilde{J}_3$
and $\tilde{J}_4$ exhibit the lowest and least structured
sigmoid activations across all layers and phases,
corroborating their fundamental unidentifiability established
in Section~\ref{sec:dl_results} and confirming that the
Xception backbone has not learned spatially distinctive
features for these parameters --- a direct consequence of
their energetic subordination to the dominant first- and
second-shell exchange. The identifiable anisotropy and field
parameters, in turn, attain their strongest activations in
the phases where they leave a measurable imprint (the
high-anisotropy Ising regime and the out-of-plane-field
configurations), consistent with their successful recovery
in the regression analysis.

\begin{figure}[H]
    \centering
    \includegraphics[width=\linewidth,
    height=0.40\textheight, keepaspectratio]
    {Figures/Regression/rams_xception_magnitude_bars.pdf}
    \caption{Mean maximum RAM activation per network
    layer and Hamiltonian parameter $\boldsymbol{\theta}$,
    averaged over $N=300$ test samples per magnetic phase
    ($N=211$ for the Ising phase; Xception CNN). Activations
    are reported after sigmoid normalization
    $\sigma\!\left(\frac{x-\mu}{\sigma_x}\right)$
    applied globally per variable, where values above
    $\sigma=0.5$ indicate above-average gradient response.
    Each color corresponds to a distinct parameter
    following the nomenclature of
    Table~\ref{tab:param_ranges}. Activation is measured
    exclusively within the nanodot disk boundary (masked
    RAM). The depth profile peaks at the terminal exit-flow
    layer \texttt{block13}, where the network aggregates the
    global representations of the identifiable parameters,
    with secondary peaks in the middle-flow layers where
    localized chiral and domain-wall features are assembled.
    The higher-order exchange constants $\tilde{J}_3$ and
    $\tilde{J}_4$ consistently exhibit the lowest sigmoid
    activation across all layers and phases, confirming the
    fundamental identifiability limit identified in the
    regression analysis.}
    \label{fig:rams_xception_bars}
\end{figure}

The RAM analysis thus isolates a physically coherent set of features as
the primary drivers of accurate regression: chiral domain-wall
boundaries, stripe periodicity, and skyrmion cores. Conversely, the
higher-order exchange constants $\tilde{J}_3$ and $\tilde{J}_4$ never
acquire spatially concentrated activations, and neither do the
anisotropy and field parameters $\tilde{K}_{an1}$, $\tilde{K}_{anS}$,
and $\tilde{H}_{ex}$ outside the specific phases where they leave an
imprint --- confirming that their limited identifiability is not a
consequence of insufficient model capacity but of the fundamental
physical constraints of the inverse problem: the energetic
subordination of the long-range exchange to the dominant first- and
second-shell interactions, and the coupling of the anisotropy and field
terms to the in-plane components $s_x$, $s_y$ that are absent from the
$s_z$ projection. For completeness, the RAM formulation was also derived
for the Vision Transformer (Section~\ref{sec:ViT_RAMs}); since Xception
is the best-performing model and provides the clearest spatial
attributions, the remainder of the interpretability analysis focuses on
it.

\subsubsection{Interpretability Benchmark: RAMs versus Perturbation-Based Attribution}
\label{sec:interpretability_benchmark_results}

Having established that RAMs isolate physically meaningful spatial
features, we now benchmark them quantitatively against the
perturbation-based attribution baselines anticipated in
Section~\ref{sec:rams_benchmark} --- LIME, SHAP (in its Kernel~SHAP
form over superpixels), and a spatial-frequency Shapley variant
(SF-SHAP) that evaluates exact Shapley values over a compact set of
radial frequency bands. The comparison targets both \emph{fidelity}
(how faithfully each map localizes the pixels that drive the
prediction) and \emph{computational cost}, and provides the empirical
support for the design argument of Objective~4: that a regression-tailored,
gradient-based attribution can match model-agnostic explainers while
remaining tractable enough to be reused as an agent-invocable capability.

\paragraph{Physically grounded segmentation.}
The perturbation baselines operate on superpixels rather than
individual pixels, so the spatial granularity of the segmentation
directly conditions their behavior. To fix this granularity on a
physical basis, the number of SLIC segments was chosen so that the
resulting superpixel equivalent diameter matches the characteristic
magnetic-domain width of the dataset ($\approx 4.9$~px). As shown in
Figure~\ref{fig:slic_criterion}, this criterion is met at $N=100$
segments, adopted for all perturbation baselines so that each
interpretable region corresponds to one coherent magnetic domain
rather than to an arbitrary pixel cluster.

\begin{figure}[H]
    \centering
    \includegraphics[width=0.8\linewidth]{Figures/Regression/slic_physical_criterion.pdf}
    \caption{Physical criterion for the SLIC segmentation used by the
    perturbation-based baselines. The superpixel equivalent diameter is
    plotted against the number of SLIC segments; the selected value
    $N=100$ (dashed red) yields superpixels whose size matches the
    characteristic magnetic domain width ($\approx 4.9$~px, dashed
    green), so that each interpretable region corresponds to a single
    magnetic domain.}
    \label{fig:slic_criterion}
\end{figure}

{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\paragraph{Faithfulness: overall and per structure.}
Fidelity is quantified with a \emph{Most Relevant First} (MoRF)
deletion protocol: the input locations are ranked by decreasing
relevance, progressively replaced by a reference baseline, and the
induced deviation of the prediction is integrated into a deletion AUC,
where a larger value indicates a more spatially localized, physically
faithful explanation (a random-relevance map serves as a lower bound).
Figure~\ref{fig:deletion_comparison} reports the aggregate deletion
curves for the three most representative parameters, and
Figure~\ref{fig:deletion_auc} resolves the corresponding AUC per
magnetic phase.

\emph{The global parameter $T^{(0)}$.} A first, physically instructive
result concerns the annealing temperature: \emph{no attribution method
separates from the random baseline} --- in fact the random control
attains the \emph{highest} deletion AUC ($0.110$, against only
$0.033$--$0.053$ for RAM, LIME, SHAP, and SF-SHAP). This is not a
deficiency of the attributions but a meaningful signature of the
variable itself. Unlike the DMI or the exchange, $T^{(0)}$ is not
focalized in any particular sector of the nanodot; it is an
\emph{ambient}, delocalized property encoded in the overall degree of
order of the entire texture. When the relevant information is spread
uniformly over the whole domain, removing a few ``most relevant''
regions perturbs the prediction less than removing pixels scattered
across the nanodot, so a random deletion order disrupts the global order
faster than any localized map. The deletion metric therefore
\emph{correctly diagnoses} $T^{(0)}$ as a non-localizable,
environment-like parameter, in clear contrast to the spatially bound
signatures of $\tilde{J}_2$ and $\tilde{K}_{DM}$, for which every
attribution method (RAMs included) rises well above random.

\emph{The localized parameters, resolved by structure.} Resolving the
deletion AUC by magnetic phase (Figure~\ref{fig:deletion_auc}) gives a
more nuanced, and deliberately honest, picture. RAMs are \emph{not}
uniformly the single most faithful method: for the strongly localized
$\tilde{K}_{DM}$ the perturbation estimators --- particularly SF-SHAP
and LIME --- frequently reach a higher deletion AUC, as expected from
methods that explicitly optimize a superpixel-level attribution at a
large query budget. What the per-phase analysis does establish is
twofold. First, RAMs are \emph{consistently competitive and of the same
order} as those far more expensive methods, and they even lead outright
in several physically meaningful regimes --- highest faithfulness for
$\tilde{J}_2$ in the ordered helical and skyrmion phases, and for
$T^{(0)}$ in the disordered \textit{others} phase. Second, and most
importantly, in the \emph{skyrmion} phase --- where the chiral cores
provide the sharpest spatial signal --- the four attribution methods for
$\tilde{K}_{DM}$ cluster tightly together and far above the random
baseline (RAM $\approx 0.20$ versus random $\approx 0.04$), so that the
RAM map is faithful to essentially the same degree as the perturbation
methods precisely where a spatial explanation is physically justified.
Conversely, in the structurally ambiguous \textit{bimeron} and
\textit{others} phases all methods drift toward the random level, which
reflects the intrinsic ill-posedness of those regimes rather than a
weakness of any particular explainer. The take-away is therefore not
that RAMs maximize faithfulness, but that they attain a faithfulness
\emph{on par with} established, purpose-built attribution methods in the
regimes where attribution is meaningful --- a near-parity that, as shown
next, is achieved at a small fraction of their computational cost.}

\begin{figure}[H]
    \centering
    \includegraphics[width=\linewidth]{Figures/Regression/deletion_comparison.pdf}
    \caption{Deletion curves (Most Relevant First) for RAM, LIME, SHAP,
    and SF-SHAP, with a random-relevance lower bound, for the three
    representative parameters $T^{(0)}$, $\tilde{J}_2$, and
    $\tilde{K}_{DM}$. The vertical axis is the induced deviation
    $|\hat{\theta}_{\text{pert}}-\hat{\theta}_0|$ as the most relevant
    pixels are progressively replaced by the baseline; the area under
    each curve (AUC, in the legend) quantifies faithfulness. For the
    localized $\tilde{K}_{DM}$ all four attribution methods lie well
    above random, whereas for the global $T^{(0)}$ the random baseline
    attains the highest AUC, reflecting that the annealing temperature
    is a delocalized, ambient property not bound to any sector of the
    nanodot.}
    \label{fig:deletion_comparison}
\end{figure}

\begin{figure}[H]
    \centering
    \includegraphics[width=\linewidth]{Figures/Regression/deletion_auc_by_structure.pdf}
    \caption{Deletion AUC resolved by magnetic phase for RAM, LIME,
    SHAP, and SF-SHAP (with the random baseline), for $T^{(0)}$,
    $\tilde{J}_2$, and $\tilde{K}_{DM}$. RAMs are competitive with, and
    of the same order as, the perturbation-based methods rather than
    uniformly superior: SF-SHAP and LIME often lead on $\tilde{K}_{DM}$,
    while RAMs lead for $\tilde{J}_2$ in the ordered helical and
    skyrmion phases and for $T^{(0)}$ in the \textit{others} phase. In
    the skyrmion phase all four attribution methods for $\tilde{K}_{DM}$
    cluster together far above the random baseline (RAM $\approx 0.20$
    versus random $\approx 0.04$), confirming faithful localization
    where the chiral signal is sharpest, whereas the delocalized
    $T^{(0)}$ shows the random baseline matching or exceeding every
    method across phases.}
    \label{fig:deletion_auc}
\end{figure}

{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\paragraph{Computational cost: the decisive advantage.}
The decisive advantage of RAMs lies in their computational cost
(Figure~\ref{fig:interp_cost}). Being gradient-based, a RAM requires
exactly one backward pass --- a single model evaluation --- whereas the
perturbation baselines must query the model over many masked
coalitions: of order $10^{3}$ evaluations for LIME and SHAP and
$2^{7}=128$ for SF-SHAP. This translates into a measured wall-clock cost
per map that is one to two orders of magnitude lower for RAMs than for
any perturbation method. RAMs therefore deliver a faithfulness
competitive with established attribution techniques while remaining
tractable for the high-throughput, per-sample, and per-layer
interpretability that a materials-characterization pipeline --- and the
agent-invocable interpreter tool of Objective~5
(Section~\ref{sec:agent_integration}) --- requires, an efficiency the
perturbation-based methods cannot match by construction.}

\begin{figure}[H]
    \centering
    \includegraphics[width=\linewidth]{Figures/Regression/interpretability_cost.pdf}
    \caption{Computational cost of the interpretability methods. Left:
    measured wall-clock time per relevance map (log scale). Right:
    hardware-independent cost as the number of model evaluations per map
    (log scale). A RAM requires a single backward pass (one model
    evaluation), against $\sim\!10^{3}$ evaluations for LIME and SHAP
    and $128$ for SF-SHAP, yielding an efficiency advantage of one to
    two orders of magnitude.}
    \label{fig:interp_cost}
\end{figure}

\subsection{Proof of Concept: Parameter-Conditioned Diffusion Model for the Forward Map}\label{sec:ddpm_poc}

As the concrete realization of the forward generator $g_{\Phi}$ introduced in Section~\ref{sec:cvae_forward}, we implemented the denoising diffusion model conditioned on the \emph{full} eight-dimensional Hamiltonian vector $\boldsymbol{\theta}$. This proof of concept instantiates the central novelty of Objective~1: instead of conditioning the generator on a single ambient scalar (the temperature), the denoiser receives the complete parameter vector that generated each configuration and must therefore learn the genuine multi-parameter map from the Hamiltonian to the magnetic texture --- precisely the regime in which parameter degeneracy arises.

\paragraph{Architecture and training.}
The denoiser is a conditional U-Net whose residual blocks receive the two conditioning signals of Equations~\ref{eq:cond_embed}--\ref{eq:cond_film}: the sinusoidal timestep embedding is injected \emph{additively}, while the eight-parameter embedding drives the per-channel affine modulation $(\boldsymbol{\gamma}^{(\ell)},\boldsymbol{\beta}^{(\ell)})$ that rescales and shifts the feature maps at every resolution. The forward diffusion uses $T=1000$ steps with a linear variance schedule, and the network is trained to predict the injected noise by minimizing the denoising objective of Equation~\ref{eq:ddpm_loss}. Optimization uses Adam ($\eta=2\times10^{-4}$) with cosine-annealing learning-rate decay and mixed-precision arithmetic; an exponential moving average (EMA, decay $0.999$) of the weights is maintained and used for sampling. The model was trained for $100$ epochs, and new configurations are generated from the parameters alone through the reverse process of Equation~\ref{eq:ddpm_sampling}.

Figure~\ref{fig:ddpm_training} reports the denoising loss over training. Both curves fall sharply within the first $\sim\!10$ epochs and plateau together near $0.058$, with the validation curve tracking the training curve throughout and no gap opening between them --- the conditional denoiser converges stably and generalizes without overfitting.

\begin{figure}[H]
    \centering
    \includegraphics[width=0.72\linewidth, trim=0 0 0 32, clip]{Figures/ddpm_film_training.pdf}
    \caption{Training and validation denoising loss (noise-prediction MSE, Equation~\ref{eq:ddpm_loss}) of the parameter-conditioned diffusion model over $100$ epochs. Both curves drop rapidly within the first ten epochs and plateau together near $0.058$ with no train--validation gap, indicating stable convergence without overfitting.}
    \label{fig:ddpm_training}
\end{figure}

\paragraph{Generation from parameters.}
Figure~\ref{fig:ddpm_samples} compares, for eight held-out parameter vectors, the original simulated configuration (top row) against a configuration generated \emph{de novo} by the diffusion model from the parameters alone (bottom row). Across morphologically distinct regimes --- helical stripes of different period and orientation, labyrinthine domains, a dense skyrmion lattice, and thermally disordered textures --- the generated samples reproduce the correct phase and the characteristic length scale dictated by $\boldsymbol{\theta}$. Because the generation is conditioned only on the parameter vector and not on the target image, this agreement is direct evidence that the full-vector conditioning steers the denoiser to the appropriate region of texture space.

\begin{figure}[H]
    \centering
    \includegraphics[width=\linewidth, trim=0 0 0 30, clip]{Figures/ddpm_film_samples.pdf}
    \caption{Original simulated configurations (top) versus configurations generated \emph{de novo} by the parameter-conditioned diffusion model from the eight-dimensional vector $\boldsymbol{\theta}$ alone (bottom), for eight held-out parameter sets. The generator reproduces the correct magnetic phase and characteristic length scale --- helical stripes, labyrinthine domains, a dense skyrmion lattice, and thermally disordered textures --- confirming that the full-vector conditioning drives the denoiser to the appropriate region of texture space.}
    \label{fig:ddpm_samples}
\end{figure}

\paragraph{Physically grounded evaluation.}
{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}Consistent with the evaluation philosophy of Objective~1, generative quality is assessed not through pixel-wise error --- which unfairly penalizes the rigid rotations and translations under which the configurations are physically equivalent --- but through rotation- and translation-invariant \emph{physical observables}. Figure~\ref{fig:ddpm_physical} contrasts two such descriptors between the real and generated ensembles: the radial spin--spin correlation function $C(r)$ and the structure factor $S(k)$. The two ensembles agree closely: the generated $C(r)$ reproduces the correlation length and the position of the first minimum near $r\approx5$~px, while the generated $S(k)$ recovers the characteristic wavevector (the shoulder near $k\approx6~\mathrm{px}^{-1}$) and the overall spectral decay, deviating only slightly at the highest wavenumbers. This demonstrates that the model captures the underlying physics of the textures --- their characteristic periodicity and correlation structure --- rather than merely their visual appearance.}

\begin{figure}[H]
    \centering
    \includegraphics[width=\linewidth, trim=0 0 0 26, clip]{Figures/ddpm_film_physical.pdf}
    \caption{Physically grounded validation of the parameter-conditioned diffusion model. Radial spin--spin correlation function $C(r)$ (left) and structure factor $S(k)$ (right) for the real (blue) and generated (red) ensembles. The generated samples reproduce the correlation length, the first minimum of $C(r)$, and the characteristic wavevector of $S(k)$, confirming that the generator captures the physical periodicity and correlation structure of the textures rather than only their pixel-level appearance.}
    \label{fig:ddpm_physical}
\end{figure}

\paragraph{Quantitative comparison against baselines.}
{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}To place this generator in context, Figure~\ref{fig:generative_metrics} compares, per magnetic phase, the relative deviation from the real data of four physical observables --- magnetization, absolute magnetization, spin correlation, and peak wavevector --- for three conditional generators: the conditional VAE baseline, a diffusion model using the reduced conditioning scheme of prior work, and our parameter-conditioned diffusion model driven by the full $\boldsymbol{\theta}$ vector. Three findings support the proposed philosophy. First, the conditional VAE deviates catastrophically on the magnetization observables, reaching $+82\%$ to $+121\%$ in the helical, \textit{others}, and bimeron phases: this is the quantitative fingerprint of a generator collapsing toward a blurred, phase-averaged texture --- exactly the degeneracy pathology the framework aims to avoid --- whereas both diffusion models stay within a few tens of percent. Second, and most tellingly, on the \emph{spin--spin correlation} --- the two-point descriptor that encodes the physical texture itself --- our full-vector diffusion model is the closest to the real data in \emph{every} phase (e.g., $-4\%$ versus $-13\%$ for the reduced-conditioning diffusion and $-26\%$ for the cVAE in the \textit{others} phase), and it likewise attains the smallest absolute-magnetization error in the ordered helical and skyrmion regimes. Third, the characteristic length scale (peak wavevector) is recovered to within a few percent by all diffusion variants, confirming that the periodicity is well captured across methods. The overall picture is consistent: conditioning on the \emph{complete} Hamiltonian vector, rather than on a single scalar or through a latent-variable bottleneck, yields generated ensembles whose physical observables --- above all the correlation structure --- best reproduce the real data across the full range of magnetic phases.}

\begin{figure}[H]
    \centering
    \includegraphics[width=\linewidth, trim=0 26 0 0, clip]{Figures/generative_metrics_by_structure_bars.pdf}
    \caption{Physically grounded comparison of conditional generators, per magnetic phase. Each panel reports the relative deviation from the real data (\%; closer to the dashed line at $0\%$ is better) of a physical observable --- magnetization, absolute magnetization, spin correlation, and peak wavevector. Bars correspond to three generators: the conditional VAE baseline (orange), a diffusion model with the reduced conditioning of prior work (\emph{DDPM-Paper}, green), and our parameter-conditioned diffusion model driven by the full eight-dimensional $\boldsymbol{\theta}$ vector (purple); the dashed line marks the real ensemble. The conditional VAE deviates by $+82\%$ to $+121\%$ on the magnetization of the disordered and modulated phases (mean-collapse signature), while our full-vector diffusion model attains the smallest spin-correlation deviation in every phase and the best absolute-magnetization agreement in the ordered helical and skyrmion regimes.}
    \label{fig:generative_metrics}
\end{figure}

Taken together, these results establish the parameter-conditioned diffusion model as the operative realization of the forward map. As anticipated in Section~\ref{sec:cvae_forward}, the conditional VAE and this diffusion generator are the two alternative candidates for $g_{\Phi}$, to be retained on the basis of empirical quality; the diffusion model was favored here because, under identical full-vector conditioning, it produced sharper and more physically consistent textures. It is this generator, conditioned on the complete Hamiltonian vector rather than a single scalar, that closes the inverse--direct cycle of Objective~1.
